May 19, 2024 05:00 PM GMT

# Global Economics Mid-Year Outlook

# Are We There Yet?

We expect the economy to be characterized by stable growth and moderate disinflation across our forecast horizon through 2025, much like in 1H24. Tight monetary policy should continue to slow growth, even as rate-cutting cycles march along.

![](images/6d3458612aa995aae975c3a74c31f7e81e8a288200d4126dfdea247e3c0f8e57.jpg)

Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result, investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley Research. Investors should consider Morgan Stanley Research as only a single factor in making their investment decision.

+= Analysts employed by non-U.S. affiliates are not registered with FINRA, may not be associated persons of the member and may not be subject to FINRA restrictions on communications with a subject company, public appearances and trading securities held by a research analyst account.

Stable growth and moderate disinflation: Since our 2024 Outlook in November, data have proven volatile to say the least. In the US, there have been a few months of downside inflation and upside growth surprises, followed by renewed inflationary pressures. In China, real growth surprised to the upside, but deflation deepened. In contrast, our two strongest-conviction bullish views – India and Japan – have played out well.

In this mid-year outlook, we lay out the case why 2024 will continue to be volatile, as the global economy struggles to shift to a slower gear, reminiscent of the mid-1990s, the last time the Fed engineered a soft landing: Because there are shared global impulses but also many idiosyncratic forces around the world, we are likely to have a bumpy ride as we decelerate.

Divergence is a returning theme – we see some slowing in the US while the rest of the world, on balance, starts to pick up: Export-led economies are seeing improvement, with European and Asian PMIs signaling a bottom turning to a moderate recovery. Indeed, we are upgrading our view of European growth in 2H24. In China, we expect supply-boosting policy to support 2024 activity even though policy is not likely to drive domestic reflation. EM ex China is more mixed in CEEMEA and Latin America, but we generally forecast stable growth.

Framing the narrative in numbers, we see global growth steady at just over 3%Y in 2024 and 2025, similar to 2023, but what is different is the mix: The US has some lingering fiscal impetus and a clear supply boost from additional immigration, allowing inflation to decelerate in 2024 and 2025 (from 4.1%Y (3.2% 4Q/4Q) in 2023 to 3.2%Y (2.9% 4Q/4Q) in 2024 and 2.2%Y (2.2% 4Q/4Q) in 2025) while growth slows but does not crash. We forecast EM growth to slow slightly in 2024 and 2025, but remain above 4%Y.

## Exhibit 3:

Gradual easing expected except for Japan

![](images/abef4ae608c27129731ec11ddc5da3c3a079c9d995aebd9de18727d7e820e722.jpg)
Source: National central banks, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

In DM central banking, we anticipate both the ECB and the BoE to cut in June on the back of soft inflation that approaches target by the end of the year. Meanwhile, the Fed follows with a policy rate cut in September, lagging because of noisy inflation data: Market expectations for Fed cuts this year have swung widely, from a 1Q start leading to almost seven cuts to expecting fewer than two cuts to somewhere in between. All of this echoes the mid-1990s cycle. We see

75bp of Fed cuts in 2024 followed by 100bp in 2025, but the path depends almost exclusively on the inflation outcome. For the ECB, we also see 75bp of cuts this year and 100bp of cuts next year, as the ECB almost reaches neutral. In contrast, the BoJ is hiking in our forecast, as inflation becomes entrenched. Across EM, we think that the degree of policy easing is limited by the level of the Fed’s policy rate. The BCB is a prime example of the requisite balancing of domestic need for easing against FX implications. EM Asian central banks should generally wait for the Fed to cut and can be patient because the smaller magnitude of their inflation and hiking cycles means that they have lesser imbalances to manage.

## Exhibit 4:

Inflation is decelerating around the world

![](images/8204197c29c8dd50f3502d083df7b022051c0a72ae80005d86472ac8a239c074.jpg)
Source: National statistical agencies, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Inflation continues to decelerate globally, and low inflation is sustained even in the context of resilient growth: Overall, while lower energy prices help headline inflation levels to ease toward 2%Y in developed economies, core inflation is a bit more inertial. We see core PCE in the US effectively at the Fed’s 2% target at the end of 2025. In Japan, we see rising inflation over the next couple of quarters before easing to just below 2%Y in 2025. The EM inflation outlook varies

considerably across regions. EM Asia continues to see a disinflationary drag from China, where we forecast sub-1%Y inflation through end-2025; it brings regional inflation levels near 2%Y over the same horizon, with India the exception. We project that Eastern Europe has inflation near target by the middle of 2025. Turkey and Egypt, as our high-inflation economies in the region, are both set to see substantial normalization over the course of 2025. In Latin America (ex Argentina), disinflation continues but at a slowing pace, and inflation eases gradually toward targets over the next 4-6 quarters.

## Growth – Resilient

Global GDP looks to hold steady at just over 3%Y, similar to 2023, except that the mix of US versus ex US growth changes hands: In the US, a strong labor market supports growth while tight monetary policy offsets the impulse. Japan’s structural shift back to nominal growth is accompanied by robust capex spending. The euro area has bottomed but faces a lingering drag from restrictive monetary policy and fiscal consolidation. In China, growth is driven by a recovery in exports and supply-oriented fiscal approach. Nevertheless, focus on deleveraging the housing sector and LGFVs continues to exert downward pressure on growth, and deflation continues.

US – we forecast a slower but still solid pace of growth: The slowdown in 2024 growth reflects constrained private demand due to tight monetary conditions as the policy rate remains higher for longer and a moderation of the fiscal impulse. The positive supply shock from immigration allows continued solid job growth and consumption, even as inflation continues to moderate. We expect GDP growth to slow from 3.1% 4Q/4Q in 2023 to 2.1% 4Q/4Q in 2024. In 2025, lower interest rates rotate spending back towards housing and goods.

Exhibit 19: Growth trends are divergent even as inflation is convergent
![](images/fba70c9aa1ce84fb1406575c2ce239ea82881bb5b13f84de1f5851b7de2578b5.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

China – the restrained mix of supply-side support amid deleveraging leaves growth sideways: A recovery in exports and modest supply-side policy have led to higher-thantarget real GDP growth in 1Q24, but a continuation of deflation. The continued focus on deleveraging in both housing and LGFV means that fiscal expansion should stay moderate, and thus real GDP growth should fall just shy of its target. We expect 4.8%Y in 2024, followed by 4.5%Y in 2025 as a debt-deflation cycle with a soft labor market continues to suppress domestic demand. However, green and strategic sectors are supported by policy.

Euro area – the economy having bottomed, modest growth acceleration in 2024 is followed by an improved 2025: Fiscal consolidation and restrictive monetary policy are key headwinds for growth, while positive real income gains on the back of a robust labor market are a support benefitting the service sector more than manufacturing. As the monetary easing cycle starts and external demand recovers, we expect modest acceleration across the forecast horizon.

Japan – broad-based wage growth with structural labor market tightness means the inflation that started with imported commodities has become a virtuous cycle of wages and inflation: Consumption spending is supported by real wage improvements. With strong US and China growth, a push towards friendshoring and reshoring, combined with a weak currency, supports exports and capex. We expect a 0.5%Y GDP growth rate in 2024, accelerating to 1.1%Y in 2025, with nominal growth well above the average of recent decades.

UK – leaving its technical recession behind: We expect growth momentum to remain solid after a 2.5%Q SAAR expansion in 1Q24. With the expected recovery in external demand as well as lower policy rates, we forecast 0.7%Y growth in 2024, improving to 1.2%Y in 2025.

India – robust domestic demand keeps growth broad-based: Consumption growth has thus far lagged capex growth in this cycle, but we expect a further recovery. Domestic demand should continue to be strong, even if softer.

Emerging markets – EM ex China is set to accelerate in 2025 as an export-led cycle complements stable domestic demand: We expect the strongest growth in India, followed by Indonesia and the Philippines. Resilient growth is also notable in Latin America, with Argentina seeing positive growth after an extended period of economic change. Eastern Europe follows the strength in Europe, and the rest of CEEMEA and MENA similarly see an improved 2025.

## Japan's economic resurgence: Will it sustain?

Japan’s economy has had a structure shift in equilibrium, away from stagnation and deflation to a nominal growth regime with a virtuous cycle in wages and prices. Decades of deflation are now over. The BoJ ended negative interest rate policy (NIRP) after eight years, and it formally abolished yield curve control (YCC). Nominal GDP growth reached 5.7%Y in 2023, exceeding that of China, an outcome which would have been unthinkable in the past. Our Japan team forecasts Japan’s nominal GDP growth at 3.7%Y in 2024. One clear market implication comes from the fact that strong nominal growth usually means strong earnings, and our equity strategy team is bullish on Japanese equities.

Japan’s rapid growth is being driven by both domestic and foreign demand, and we believe it is outstripping potential growth. Consequently, we see a positive output gap for the first time in four years. This fact is sustaining inflation that had started primarily through imported commodities inflation. Japan-style core CPI, the focus of the BoJ, has generally exceeded the 2%Y target since April 2022. Moreover, the labor market remains tight, with the BoJ’s Tankan survey showing persistent employment shortages, particularly in the service sector. Labor supply is also rising, as women’s labor participation rate has risen and now has surpassed that of the US. Unsurprisingly, wage inflation has picked up. The spring wage negotiations for 2024 exceeded 5% the first time in 33 years. We have entered a virtuous feedback loop between prices and wages.

The positive feedback loop is broader, with a sustained surge in corporate profits allowing the wage gains to continue. These profits and the tight labor market have also spurred robust capital investment, further reinforcing the strong growth. In addition, exports, in part supported by industrial policy for semiconductors, are another future strength. And the defense industry, hitherto largely untapped, has scope to expand and increase exports following the partial revision of the guidelines of the Defense Equipment Transfer Act.

However, despite all these drivers, consumption spending continues to be lackluster, and it remains the risk to sustained reflation. This risk is reinforced by demographic headwinds. In our base case, we anticipate gradual strengthening in consumption as wage increases continue and spread. Moreover, a forthcoming small income tax cut starting in June should provide further support. Despite the persistently low population growth rate, Japan’s readiness to embrace immigrants is expected to partially offset the decline.

Putting all the pieces together, on net we expect continued strong growth, with inflation remaining near the BoJ’s target. We look for a further rate hike of 15bp in July 2024 and 25bp in January 2025. Even that setting of policy would leave a negative real policy rate, so we see risks as skewed toward more hikes than the baseline, in the event that inflation surprises to the upside.

Exhibit 20: Wage trends are showing a step change by rising higher, and the defense industry has scope to expand in both domestic spending and exports

![](images/a4b75578bf146bbf4c03a053d83e2b9b443967e476293eea999d8a853a3cefea.jpg)

![](images/444c30e23f30bdfcfb5197bb574489ae048dd8552c551f0484fd1e17945004ed.jpg)
Source: MHLW, Rengo, Ministry of Defense, Cabinet Office, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: We forecast that the base-up portion is 3.2% in the final tabulation of 2024 Spring Wage negotiations.

## Inflation – Easy Does it

Headline inflation moderated as commodity prices stabilized throughout 1Q24. Inflationary pressures in the US are on a downward trajectory and we expect inflation to reach close to the Fed’s target by 2025. In the euro area, disinflation is faster, and inflation hits the ECB’s target by 1Q25. Meanwhile, in China, GDP deflation persists in our forecast, and given the supply-oriented policy, inflation is likely to remain subdued even in 2025. In Japan, the longawaited virtuous cycle of wages and inflation is materializing.

Exhibit 21: Inflation has been decelerating globally
![](images/52e5bab3e56f2d48ded7cd3361fe164595c53e6dcd90799dafa3cc24c85b09c7.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts; Note: CEEMEA excludes Ukraine, Turkey, and Egypt, LatAm excludes Argentina.

In the US, despite noise in 1Q24, core services inflation is set to fall. We expect a faster pace of rent disinflation in 2H24, a key contribution to the stalled deceleration in 1H. Core goods deflation continues, but gradually returns to normal in 2025.

In the euro area, the disinflation trend is

Exhibit 22: Data on rents continue to point to a deceleration in US shelter inflation in 2H24

![](images/c7a39986d2fef52d45e9437af0da470887fb3d997468336b0c35ecd6195576bc.jpg)
Source: BLS, Morgan Stanley Researc

clear, and we expect core inflation to be close to target in 2025. Disinflation of headline inflation should be bumpy because of energy prices. In the euro area, we expect headline inflation at 2.4%Y in 2024 and 2.1%Y in 2025.

The UK is also expected to see inflation converge back to target in 2024, as early as 2Q.
The pace of disinflation accelerates at the turn of the year.

We believe that Japan has fully exited its deflationary equilibrium, and the virtuous cycle between wages and prices means a higher nominal equilibrium. Japan core inflation should remain well above the BoJ’s 2%Y target in 2024, and close to it in 2025.

In Australia, a goods-led disinflationary path should continue through 2024, moving inflation back into the RBA’s 2-3%Y target band later in 2025.

While China may get out of deflation, we think that very low inflation persists because policy will not be aimed at demand, but rather at supporting supply in key industries and furthering deleveraging. We expect CPI to remain subdued in both 2024 and 2025 with the GDP deflator recovering to a low level from -0.3% in 2024 to 0.4% in 2025.

Exhibit 23: Disinflation continues in DMs, but the last mile of the inflation journey – normalizing core inflation – is a gradual one
![](images/c56f8b0b86022977d19d5e42cd6654af4a47aa4e86ea6767b6cd91847918bfa5.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts; Note: G4 includes US, Euro Area, Japan and UK. US core inflation is core PCE inflation. ^ Japan's core CPI is excluding energy and fresh food, and ex VAT and Free child education impact.

Exhibit 24: EM inflation is more vulnerable to oil price shocks, while food inflation shocks remain a concern in India and Brazil
![](images/9815fba91543287f264e8e0587c0385f4ee60b78850fbc44651bdb801cfa37a7.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts; Note; Selected EM includes China, India, Brazil, and Mexico; the CPI weights used for China are based on the imputed Bloomberg weights.

EM Asia broadly has inflation trending close to target, with central banks reacting more to the threat of inflation than measured inflation. India has the highest inflation in the EM Asia universe, though not a surprise given the strong growth in the region, but even there we remain close to the target band. Specifically, softening continues in core CPI as supplyside normalization continues, while sticky food price should keep headline inflation volatile and high.

LatAm should continue to see disinflation, albeit at a slowing pace, throughout 2024 and 2025. Argentina is one exception where inflation accelerated in 1H24, but we see price pressures fading meaningfully through 2025, driven by weak consumption and currency appreciation.

In CE3 economies, disinflation would be driven mainly by goods while services would keep service inflation somewhat persistent in the near term, but decelerations are expected toward the target throughout 2025. Elsewhere in CEEMEA, goods-led deceleration would broadly continue. In Ukraine, a saturated agriculture market resulted in sharp disinflation in early 2024, with inflation undershooting the 5%Y target, but gradual UAH depreciation would accelerate inflation in 2025. The stability in the currency and easing supply constraints imply a gradual downtrend of Egypt’s inflation. Slowing domestic demand would lead to declining inflation in Turkey. In South Africa, inflation pressure should moderate due to tight monetary policy. In Saudi Arabia and Israel, we see inflation

## remaining subdued.

The last outlook was about the journey to normalization; in the months since then notable progress has been made, and we anticipate that DM central banks will be ready to kick off cutting cycles soon. However, the journey to target inflation is not over yet. In our forecasts, most countries still have at least one year before inflation converges. EM central banks, overall, remain well positioned. They have demonstrated a cautious approach in this bumpy path to normalization.

## Deflationary spillovers from China

China is in its longest and deepest deflation since the 1990s, raising the question of whether the deflationary impulse will be exported around the world. Regression analysis using data from 2009 to 2019 that accounts for exchange rate movements confirms spillovers to domestic price indexes for the US and the euro area for consumer goods prices, whereas for Japan, the spillover is limited to import price indexes. In particular, Chinese consumer goods PPI appears to give the best signal.

Exhibit 25: The pass-through of Chinese consumer goods PPI into core indices is small for the 2009-19 period

![](images/54e8bcc5538274faa9fd26795bca91fb532039764115dce20f200f140ffdbd5d.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research

Our results suggest that a 1pp decline in Chinese consumer goods PPI leads to a maximum 0.52pp decline in US core goods prices after nine months. For US core CPI, the effect is a decline of 0.07pp, largely reflecting the share of goods in core. Over the past year, this Chinese index has ranged between -0.2% and -1.3%Y. Taking lagged effects into account, the initial deflation in Chinese consumer goods PPI in June 2023 would lead to a 0.25pp fall in US goods prices over the next three quarters. With the latest reading still in negative territory, we expect this pressure on US inflation to continue.

In the euro area, the effect on HICP goods is slightly stronger, with a 1pp fall in Chinese PPI leading to a 0.61pp fall in HICP goods, and a 0.11pp fall in core HICP after nine months. For the euro area, the Chinese PPI deflation in June 2023 would lead to roughly a 0.30pp decline in HICP goods inflation over three quarters.

The results suggest that while there is and will continue to be some downward pressure on core goods prices, the net effects on core inflation measures are much smaller than the deflation in China and occur with some lag. Adding to the imprecision, goods prices have already been falling in the US and euro area; the pass-throughs of Chinese deflationary pressures can contribute to this deceleration, but the effect is within the uncertainty of the overall deflation in goods from healed supply chains and as consumption shifts back to services.

## Fiscal – Running to Stand Still...Still

Our 2024 fiscal deficit forecasts are largely consolidating in line with the post-Covid trend. The US is an exception, where we forecast the deficit to be slightly wider in 2025 than 2024, though not by much, and therefore we are not expecting a material fiscal impulse. The US fiscal impulse, which has been negative for 2024, would broadly neutralize in 2025 on our forecasts. We see government consumption growth decelerating through the forecast horizon, remaining below 1% for the duration of 2025.

The euro area and UK continue to see fiscal consolidation, with the UK just barely falling short of a primary surplus. However, the government spending growth impulse in the euro area is likely to remain stronger even as the deficit is narrowing more quickly because of lagged investment spend. We see government consumption growth at approximately 1%Y through 2025. Poland is perhaps a good example of the benefit of lagged fiscal impulse as EU accession funds saw us revise up the growth outlook recently.

In emerging markets, we also forecast broad-based fiscal consolidation in 2025, even in China. Notably in China and Mexico, we see the positive fiscal impulse turning negative, which is a contrast to other EM economies where fiscal balances have largely been trending tighter for a few years now. The weaker fiscal impulse in China is linked to the softening growth outlook we see there as well as the lack of an inflationary impulse. Fiscal consolidation efforts in Argentina and Egypt will be in particular focus, though in both cases we forecast substantive improvements on the back of reform progress.

## DSA framework for DM sovereign debt

Our recent deep dive into DM sovereign debt and fiscal paths analyzed debt sustainability through a debt-sustainability analysis (DSA) framework. The framework provides calculations on how the interaction of interest rates, nominal growth, and fiscal deficits affect debt stability. The math converges to show estimates for the debt-stabilizing primary balance (DSPB), the level where the interest cost times the size of the debt is growing in line with the economy. Most countries have deficit levels in 2025 that are forecast to be within the DSPB, though the US, UK, and France are exceptions where deficits remain wider.

Exhibit 26: Baseline DM sovereign debt forecasts are trending sideways currently
![](images/996c12d92f523536df7939899abe99e3f15b37fc4cd1b81c520fadb992bb28bb.jpg)
Source: National statistical agencies, Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: The composition of global debt/GDP is the Morgan Stanley DM coverage universe: US, Canada, Japan, UK, Germany, France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, and Australia. We use gross debt/GDP, attempting to standardize debt obligations close to IMF standards but using national sources; for countries with tangible sovereign linked sets or funds, these are generally not counted to offset debt/GDP. Where there are differences in convention of estimation, we make note of the standardized approach across countries.

Immediately after Covid, high nominal growth rates helped to keep debt ratios in check. As nominal growth declines and interest rates have risen, the fiscal balance will drive debt stability. As debt matures and is rolled over, debt-servicing costs eventually converge to market rates. We find that fiscal balances will likely inflect over the next 3-5 years through that catch-up.

Roughly projecting the rates implied by forward curves, interest expenses as a share of GDP globally will rise from \~2.5% to \~3.5%, on average, by the end of the decade. To hold debt stable, smaller primary deficits or higher primary surpluses would be needed. Primary fiscal deficits currently average about -2.5% versus -0.75% in the two years before Covid.

While fiscal trends have recently trended in the right direction, higher interest costs mean that primary fiscal deficits have further to go for debt to stabilize in most countries over the medium term. If a primary fiscal balance is not achieved by 2030, the baseline DSA model shows debt/GDP at risk of accelerating.

Exhibit 27: Risks to sovereign debt trajectories rise when the cost of debt reprices to market levels. As interest payments rise, running a primary surplus becomes harder
![](images/fc6395aefa36291191b104c823d09ce58cb7430af62fe0ce80e4ac6d3208594f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, national statistical agencies, Haver Analytics, national debt offices, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/43b88054b914d9c9539f83a8effb40e6491ce5ac0baf1b22c34df06687c628fb.jpg)

## Monetary Policy – Let the Cuts Begin

We see DM cutting cycles starting imminently. Europe should start, with the ECB and the BoE in June, then the Fed lags by a quarter because of recent noisy inflation data. Recent European inflation has been clearer. The outlier is Japan, where negative policy rates have been left behind and policy normalizes, with the BoJ hiking as other DM central banks cut. Emerging markets are generally balancing external pressures from the high level of US policy rates against a need to lower rates – especially real rates – for domestic objectives, but the relative importance of these factors varies across the globe.

Fed: We see three cuts in 2024 and four in 2025, with a move at each meeting starting in September and ending June next year. Core inflation at 2.7% Q4/Q4 is enough for the Fed to cut three times in 2024 because the downward path will be evident. We see inflation slightly above target through end-2025, and with real growth solid, the Fed is left to assess “neutral.” We see QT ending around 1Q25.

ECB: The ECB is set to start cutting in June. Despite starting to hike later, it is cutting sooner because growth has been weak and inflation has fallen more convincingly. In our forecast, the ECB cuts quarterly through the end of 2025, ending just a hair above its estimate of neutral, but needing to lean a bit against the higher level of rates in the US.

BoE: The BoE follows a similar path to the ECB but the sequence is slightly different as we anticipate two consecutive cuts and then a pause before a final cut to round out 2024 for a total of 75bp. In 2025 we see cuts between February and August, before a pause at 3.25% after 125bp of cuts. We forecast inflation to be at target for much of 2025.

BoJ: After the removal of both the negative interest rate policy and yield curve control in March, we see a move into positive territory. We expect a small 15bp hike in July followed by a 25bp hike in 1Q25 as inflation expectations solidify in the Japanese economy.

EM Asia: As China grapples with deflation and holds rates low over the forecast horizon, we see a very modest easing path across the region. Most central banks do not move ahead of the Fed, and higher US rates limit the possibility for cuts. On net most EM Asia central banks see little change, particularly in 2025. For example, we forecast the Reserve Bank of India to stay at 6.5%.

LatAm: Higher for longer sees a shallower path in Latin America, particularly in 2025 where we see most policy rates holding relatively steady. We forecast Brazil to get to 10.0% at the end of 2024 and then stay there, and Mexico to eventually lower rates to 9.75% at the end of this year.

CE3: The cutting cycles are already in progress in CE3, though the pace differs. We see quick and aggressive cuts in Hungary and the Czech Republic in 2H24 before finding a neutral rate in the middle of 2025. Meanwhile, in Poland, we see light cuts across the horizon, following the ECB relatively closely.

SARB: We expect the SARB to cut in the back half of the year as the Fed begins but to hold constant at 7.25% in 2025.

CBT: We forecast rates on hold at 50% until 4Q, when we see inflation falling below 50%Y to enable rates to follow. The rate path through 2025 sees steady cuts through the year, ending with rates and inflation at similar levels; we see interest rates at 29% in 4Q25.

CBE: After hikes following the IMF package and currency move, we see interest rates rising to tackle inflation, reaching 27.25% in 3Q before holding for a year, at which point Egypt starts to cut slowly as inflation passes its peak.

## Finding a new equilibrium

Central bank balance sheets have become pivotal in monetary policy since the global financial crisis. In The Great Unwind we unpack key issues with central bank balance sheets in the current environment. Quantitative tightening (QT) is under way across most central banks, with the Fed recently announcing a taper. In the note, we had four main conclusions that remain relevant:

First, QT is not the opposite of QE. QE is a policy tool to ease financial conditions during times of stress and when policy rates were at the effective lower bound. Central banks were trying to signal a commitment to ease, stimulate borrowing, boost credit supply, and lower longer-term rates. QT happens away from the zero lower bound, and continues even as central banks are contemplating rate cuts, so no signaling is involved. And because most QT is passive, central banks are not directly intervening in markets, further muting the effect.

Second, central bank balance sheets differ across economies. For example, the US is a capital markets-focused economy, and most of the balance sheet expansion came through purchases of Treasuries and MBS. The euro area, by contrast, is much more bank dependent, so in addition to QE the ECB deployed targeted longer-term refinancing operations (TLTROs) in direct funding through banks.

Third, central banks can clearly experience losses. As assets pay a fixed coupon and liabilities are tied to policy rates, the consequence of higher interest rates is negative net income. This is not unprecedented and, in fact, central banks can function even with negative equity for long periods of time. In general, central bank P&Ls lack macroeconomic implications, although political considerations can accrue over time.

Fourth, balance sheets will shrink substantially but should not revert to past levels. Larger balance sheets are the new normal, and both the Fed and the ECB have codified 'floor systems' in their plans. While central bank balance sheets have more to contract, we should not get back to pre-GFC levels.

Central bank balance sheets have contracted by \~US\$5 trillion since the peak with an additional contraction of \~US\$4 trillion forecast through 2025, the end of our forecast horizon.

Exhibit 34: Central bank balance sheets are declining
![](images/a2e7100adfde723fdbe16d99f86596b215af1ef55ae5b91250b357f61c5e21fa.jpg)

## Ripple effects

EM central banks were early to respond to rising inflation, keeping real rates at historically high levels. Over time, as inflation began to turn, we saw the first steps toward an easing cycle in the EM universe. Thus far this year, a stronger US economy and volatile inflation are delaying the Fed cutting cycle. For EMs, this brings a mix of stronger export demand and risks of currency weakness, which can lead to stronger growth and/or inflation. EM central bank easing cycles depend on the Fed.

Steep rate hikes early in the cycle and an equally steep fall in inflation have produced elevated real rates across EMs. On a 12- month forward expected CPI basis, real rates are higher than pre-Covid levels across EMs, with LatAm and CEEMEA facing highly restrictive real rates. To provide some respite from elevated real rates, most CEEMEA and LatAm central banks started easing cycles, but real rates are averaging 3.4pp and 2.3pp above their pre-Covid levels in LatAm and CEEMEA, respectively.

Exhibit 35: EM central banks hiked rates early, and most EMs have seen inflation converge to toward their targets. However, this has led to real rates that are higher than pre-Covid levels

![](images/3e32034185656115fa81ef80e3a917d46e2b0a03b9f9e2c2848ef017fdd25580.jpg)

![](images/24aad1ad18f4005cc9a8df4bb79d323133c63b3b10f0658f242e8ce8aff1b56c.jpg)
Source: National sources, Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts; \*In the case of Korea and Taiwan, central banks have inflation targets but not ranges. We use +/-1% ranges around the inflation target for representation purposes.

Over the last six months, across EMs disinflation has largely been on track, with inflation entering the target ranges of most central banks. For EMs not yet at target, the gap to the target range is narrowing and price stability appears to be within sight. In several economies including India and Brazil, underlying details of inflation dynamics have been encouraging. Core inflation has also slowed meaningfully, especially in Asia, where it is tracking at or below target in almost all economies.

Despite the strong progress on inflation, we are seeing changing communications across the EM universe as rate expectations in the US were pushed out. We saw Bank Indonesia react with an unexpected hike and we have similarly seen greater caution from other EM central banks. As far away as Brazil we saw a shift in the tone of communication. These changes demonstrate to us the willingness of EM central banks to defend higher real rates.

The headwind to growth from elevated real rates is partially offset by tailwinds of improving external demand. Indeed, we see an acceleration in global exports in 2024. If the Fed were to delay rate cuts due to upside surprises to inflation and not better growth prospects, it would be a clear negative for EMs. However, for now, we see stable and robust growth prospects for EMs despite the 'higher for longer' environment.

Egypt represents fiscal year from July 1 to June 30

## Alternative Scenarios

## Upside scenario – a fundamentally stronger Chinese economy

China is one of the biggest economies in the world and has a persistent shortfall in domestic demand. A surge there would be a notable surprise. In that scenario, we would imagine that the debt-deflation loop is short-circuited due to a combination of factors: increased domestic consumption demand which supports CPI; government support for the liquidity stress in the property sector, boosting consumer confidence further; growth in prices in export-related sectors of manufacturing and green capex exceeds expectations due to robust global demand; local government bond issuance increases more than expected due to policy changes and this supports domestic credit growth; and support for consumption for lowerincome households. As nominal GDP recovers, industry profit margins begin to recover and wage growth resumes. Consumer confidence recovers and a virtuous loop is created.

In China, the push by policymakers to rebalance toward consumption leads to faster and more sustainable reflation. Real GDP growth could reach 5.3-5.5%Y in 2024-25, and the GDP deflator recovers to 0.5% and 2.5%, respectively, pushing up nominal GDP growth.

For the US, the more resilient Chinese economy feeds into higher import prices in the US, and the subsequent pass-through to core goods prices raises core PCE inflation by twotenths relative to the baseline. Higher prices also weigh on domestic demand. The first Fed cut is pushed to the end of 2024, and we continue to see 100bp of cuts in 2025. Despite stronger Chinese growth boosting global growth, the Fed would still rein in US growth and inflation, creating divergence.

In the euro area, stronger export demand from a more robust Chinese economy boosts GDP growth. Combined with higher oil prices, euro area inflation deceleration pauses, and it persists at a higher level. After the first two cuts in June and September 2024, the ECB stops its easing cycle. A similar transmission channel in the UK leads the BoE to stop cutting after the first two cuts in June and August.

Stronger growth of exports and a weak yen create upside risks to inflation in Japan. In this case, there is a risk of the BoJ hiking again in July 2025, pushing the policy rate to 0.75%.

## Downside scenario – persistent inflation

This envisions a sooner end to the immigration surge and thus more persistent services inflation in the US, which is an idiosyncratic, domestic US driver. This stagflationary outcome leads the Fed to stay higher for longer. With the resulting slower US growth, the spillover to the rest of the world is through both higher real rates for EM and lower US growth. US growth is at or just below 1% in 2025, with the lagged effects of tighter Fed policy through 2024 and beyond. This idiosyncratic component of inflation does not imply more inflation elsewhere, so disinflation continues, particularly in EM. EM central banks are forced to delay or reduce their easing cycles because of higher US rates, which leads to a double whammy – slower growth because of the US and yet still higher domestic real rates.

The US team expects that this downside scenario would ignite recession fears and the initial bout of inflation fuels stagflation speculation. But as policy stays tighter for longer, slack develops in the economy and inflation begins to move lower towards 2.5%. The Fed begins to cut rates in June 2025 to lower real rates, with 150bp in cuts over 2H25.

In this scenario, the ECB still cuts in June and September 2024. However, the higher Fed path and resulting exchange rate pressures push euro area inflation up, and the ECB pauses at 3.5%. In 2025, restrictive monetary policy and lower US demand weigh on euro area GDP, and euro area inflation risks undershooting the target. This outcome eventually leads the ECB to resume its easing cycle, with 50bp cuts in 2H25. In the UK, while the BoE still cuts in June and September 2024, FX considerations lead to a pause in the easing cycle. By 2Q25, the BoE resumes policy rate cuts, as restrictive monetary policy and lower US demand weigh on GDP and inflation falls below the baseline scenario.

For Japan, a slowing US economy and external demand, and weakening domestic inflation could push the BoJ to lower the policy rate in 2025. In the next recession phase, we expect the BoJ to implement quantitative easing by increasing its JGB purchases, rather than returning to the negative interest rate policy (NIRP).

## Additional risk scenarios

In addition to the global upside and downside scenarios, our economists have highlighted other risks in their regional outlooks. We note three key risks here:

1) What gets the Fed to hike? We and the markets expect the Fed to cut this year. Recent inflation data reinforce that view. But the persistent question is what could lead to the next move being a hike? Fundamentally, such an outcome would require a shift in the disinflation path where inflation starts to rise again. An abrupt end to the positive labor supply boost from immigration could be such a catalyst. Alternately, categories of prices such as healthcare services or consumer goods could reverse course.

2) Geopolitical risks: Our CEEMEA economists note that the duration, scope, and intensity of the conflicts in Ukraine and the Middle East remain major sources of uncertainty to the growth outlook in that region.

Exhibit 52: Key upcoming global elections in 2024
<table><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Date</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Event</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>29 May 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>South African general election</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>02 June 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Mexican general election</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>04 June 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Indian general election (held in 7 phases starting on 19 April)</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>09 June 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>European Parliament election</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>06 October 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Brazil Municipal Elections</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>05 November 2024</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>US elections</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>January 2025</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>UK general elections *Risks of earlier election</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>October 2025</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Japan general elections *Risks of earlier election</td></tr></table>

Source: Morgan Stanley Research

3) Elections around the world: There are key elections coming up around the world as noted in Exhibit 52 . In June, the results from the Mexican, Indian, and European elections will come in. Our euro area colleagues note that the European Parliament elections are expected to have limited impact on EU legislation. There is a risk of earlier general elections in the UK. For the US, the post-election deficit path is fairly uncertain, and both the Democratic and Republican parties have achieved most of their recent policy priorities, a fact that could limit the size of deficit increases. Moreover, a split federal government could lead to political stalemate, preventing much shift in fiscal policy. Tariffs or other executive branch authorities could also have a macro impact, but the details will be critical.

Country Snapshots

US: Solid Growth, but Increasing Slack – Rapid immigration supports faster employment and output growth, even as tight monetary policy contributes to growth moderation. We forecast real GDP growth of 2.1% 4Q/4Q in 2024 and 2.1% in 2025, while the unemployment rate increases to 4.2% and 4.5%, respectively. Inflation slows near target after noisy 1Q24 data. Three 25bp cuts this year (starting September) and four next year lower the policy rate to 4.625% in 4Q24 and 3.625% in 4Q25.

Euro Area: Reaching (Low) Potential – Growth has bottomed and should be 0.7%Y in 2024 and 1.2%Y in 2025, after 0.5%Y in 2023. Private consumption is strengthening, while we expect exports to pick up. But potential growth remains dampened by structural challenges. We expect inflation to recede further, with headline and core stabilizing around 2%Y in early 2025. We think that the ECB will start cutting in June (-25bp), for a total of 75bp cuts in 2024 and 100bp in 2025.

Japan: The Year of Normalization – The Japanese economy has been shifting equilibria to an economy with positive nominal GDP growth with steady real growth and a sustained inflation and wage cycle. Domestic capex should exceed its bubble-era peak, partly due to gains from friendshoring/reshoring. We expect the BoJ to normalize rates policy further and reduce JGB purchases at a measured pace.

UK: When the Rubber Meets the Road – The next six months should provide concrete answers on the role of the (now dissipating) energy shock, or if structural shifts in recent years have left a permanently tighter economy. We have long advocated the former view. Core inflation should follow the energy-driven decline in headline figures, with growth firming as the BoE removes restrictiveness, with Bank Rate at 4.5% in December 2024 and 3.25% in December 2025.

China: Steady Yet Subdued – Real GDP growth has stabilized on exports and manufacturing capex, but we think that nominal GDP growth will remain below consensus at a subdued level (4.5%Y in 2024 and 4.9%Y in 2025), as the current policy approach of pushing investment in manufacturing rather than boosting consumption means persistent deflationary pressure.

India: Strength and Stability in the Economy – Robust demand keeps domestic growth at or above 6.5%Y over the next two years. We expect the cycle to run longer, driven by productivity-enhancing growth keeping macro stability risks at bay. Risks emerge from the general election outcome or cyclical risks from external factors.

Brazil: Consumers in the Driver's Seat – We see growth at 2.3%Y in 2024 and 1.6%Y in 2025, with the difference primarily from the private consumption backdrop. We expect inflation to decelerate further, albeit at a slower pace given resilient core services and a strong labor market. We think the BCB will stop the easing cycle at 10% with two more cuts of 25bp this year and keep rates unchanged throughout 2025.

# US: Solid Growth, but Increasing Slack

Ellen Zentner

(1 212) 296 4882

US Economics Team

Exhibit 53: Our GDP forecast is much stronger than last November
![](images/79542b184854506858cf37a72c9ea3d23f8950b411f82af3884e6925724fecc9.jpg)
Source: BEA, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 54: Faster immigration has increased the size of the economy
![](images/db896309e8a470ea9898bc66072a6de2d4a49d61e1ebb5e7f159ccfc57167052.jpg)
Source: CBO, Morgan Stanley Research

Rapid immigration supports faster employment and output growth, even as tight monetary policy contributes to a moderation in the economy. We forecast real GDP growth of 2.1% 4Q/4Q this year and next year, while the unemployment rate increases by 0.5pp relative to 4Q23 to 4.2% this year and further to 4.5% next year. Inflation slows to near the Fed target after the noisy data of 1Q24. Fed rate cuts begin in September. Three 25bp cuts this year and four next year lower the policy rate to 4.625% in 4Q24 and 3.625% in 4Q25.

Bigger, not tighter: An intellectual conundrum has been resolved. Faster growth in the US labor force than we recognized in our 2024 Outlook explains last year's combination of rapid growth and decelerating (price and wage) inflation. Data so far suggest that this year's immigration flows will be similar to last year's. Since our 2024 Outlook, we revised our forecasts significantly: the increased labor supply allows for faster growth and more slack than we had forecast. With faster employment growth, incomes and consumption grow faster; and the stronger outlook for spending supports solid business investment. We have revised up our real GDP growth estimates by about two-thirds of a percentage point in 2024 and 2025. We also revised up the path of the unemployment rate, not because labor demand falls notably, but because labor supply is rising.

Current monetary policy is tight, and fiscal policy's boost moderates this year: Despite growth above 2% this year and next, slack in the economy increases: the unemployment rate rises by 0.6pp between now, April 2024, and the end of 2025; inflation slows roughly to target.

Signs of resumed disinflation allow the Fed to begin rate cuts in September. Disinflation combined with rising labor market slack continues the cuts. By mid-2025, the Fed pauses to reassess because domestic demand remains solid and inflation is hovering around target.

In fiscal policy, direct government spending swings from a 0.75pp contribution to growth in 2023 to near zero in 2024. We assume little change to that impulse in 2025, given election uncertainty.

Consumer spending slows in the remainder of this year as the saving rate rises about 0.5pp, reverting toward historical averages, and goods spending continues to be restrained by high interest rates. By mid-2025, the restraint from rates moderates, so wallet share shifts toward goods. Business investment slows somewhat this year as the impulse from fiscal policy for infrastructure investment steps down. But by next year, pent-up demand and less monetary restraint contribute to a pick-up. After several years of weakness in equipment investment, increased immigration and labor input of the past couple years pressure the capital stock, and business spending responds.

Exhibit 55: Domestic demand slows and reaccelerates more than GDP
![](images/70837be2938d80e84783700f604a055dba415e41d24b538c59d8697e55d9f96c.jpg)
Source: BEA, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 56: Inflation moving to target
![](images/5c448278019b6b1122e917a5b626cc859665bcfb7defb21b3ff69a5ac3ecc721.jpg)
Source: BEA, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Our forecast has GDP growth moderating in 2024 and 2025 and running a bit below the near-term potential growth rate of the economy, given the likely temporary boost to the labor market from immigration. But despite the slowing, growth remains solid in both real and nominal terms.

Inflation converges to the Fed's target in our forecast. Reflation in 1Q24 was notable, but the main sources of pressure – goods and financial services – were idiosyncratic. Where the signal is clearest, it points to disinflation, and where the data show reflation, they are noisy. Our analysis also suggests that 1Q inflation was partly driven by residual seasonality, pointing to payback in 2H24.

Beginning with the April print, we expect monthly inflation reports consistent with further rapid disinflation. In our forecast, core PCE inflation slows to a 2.7% annual rate in the three months through June, 2.1% through September, and 1.8% through December. The month/month inflation run-rate remains at or below the Fed's target through 2025.

On a 4Q/4Q basis, we expect core PCE inflation to slow from 3.2% last year to 2.7% this year and 2.1% in 2025.

Monetary policy remains a drag: The noisy 1Q inflation data delayed Fed easing, but with renewed evidence of disinflation, we expect the first cut in September. The continued rise in unemployment across our forecast emphasizes the risks of restrictive policy and leads to additional easing in 1H25. Three 25bp rate cuts this year and four next year leave the fed funds rate at 3.625% in mid-2025. At that point, the Fed pauses cuts to assess the medium-term state of the economy, with robust growth but inflation at target.

# Euro Area: Reaching (Low) Potential

Jens Eisenschmidt (49 69) 2166 2817

Exhibit 58: Private consumption should be key for growth in 2024-25
![](images/201edf398651acbfe5f149a93f81ab2e989c8daa52955674b5d85e1f32a7038e.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 59: Momentum strengthens in 2024
![](images/cfb1f3d1ca5b22673f1ca4763f243c81dab6cf0fb9e38871f0e8b031ffd5b905.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

European Economics Team

Growth has bottomed and should be 0.7%Y in 2024 and 1.2%Y in 2025, after 0.5%Y in 2023. Private consumption is strengthening on the back of real income gains, while we expect exports to pick up as global trade rebounds. But potential growth remains dampened by structural challenges (demographics, productivity, energy transition). We expect inflation to recede further, with headline and core stabilizing around 2%Y in early 2025. We think that the ECB will start cutting in June (-25bp), for a total of 75bp cuts in 2024 and 100bp in 2025.

Growth | Recovering in 2024... Despite similar annual growth figures, 2024 should be very different from 2023. GDP was broadly flat throughout last year, even declining in the second half. On the contrary, annual growth in 2024 should come from GDP picking up steadily. At the end of 2024, the momentum (1.1% 4Q/4Q in 2024) should be stronger than the annual figures suggest.

…with private consumption in the driver's seat: On the back of real income gains, private consumption has picked up. After a weak 2023, the recovery of exports should gain momentum in the second half of the year. Stronger domestic and external demand should then start to push productive investment up. Ultimately, the only weak spot at the end of the year is likely construction, where the impact of restrictive monetary policy continues to be felt.

Where is the limit? Stronger growth throughout 2024 should pave the way for a further uptick in 2025 in annual terms. Next year will likely be about whether the euro area sees enough positive impulses to grow above potential, which we estimate to be subdued, at 1.0%Y. This will probably not come from domestic demand because of remaining lagged effects of restrictive monetary policy and fiscal consolidation in some countries. The positive impulse taking euro area growth above potential will likely have to come from the external side.

Inflation | Target in sight: Inflation has declined notably from its peak of 10.6%Y in October 2022, reaching 2.4%Y in April 2024. Core inflation, at 2.7%Y in April 2024, has more than halved since its peak of 5.6%Y in June 2023. Disinflation should continue in 2024 and 2025, albeit at a slower speed. The key factor behind the sluggish decline of core is robust momentum in labour-intensive services. But, as wage growth is expected to have peaked in 2023, services inflation should continue to recede. The path for headline should be affected by the bumpy profile for energy inflation: after some swings up and down, and even possibly a brief drop below 2.0%Y in September 2024, headline inflation should be around the ECB’s target from 1Q25 onwards.

Exhibit 60: Inflation converges to 2%Y
![](images/46450ee037e340dcbc5490cbdffac869f0e4264d5e52b40ac736828d0ad02d5d.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 61: ECB path
![](images/a0b421ec0bf78c98e2dc29bbc418430c091e3e0a89f69542dd4c5a8e2876ade0.jpg)
Source: ECB, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Fiscal policy | Consolidating slowly: We expect the fiscal impulse to turn negative in 2024-25 as we project a mild reduction of the euro area's public deficit in both years. The voted reform of the Stability and Growth Pact does not change the perspective that numerous countries, including France and Italy, are likely to face an Excessive Deficit Procedure (EDP) this summer. This means that they will be required to take significant steps toward the 3% target.

ECB | First cut in June... A clear hint of an imminent cut was given at the April meeting and we expect the ECB to cut rates by 25bp in June. The ECB has not committed to a particular rate path afterwards and we anticipate a gradual approach as it will have to observe whether developments in wages, profits, and productivity support the inflation outlook and hence further cuts. This leads us to expect cuts only at projection meetings, and we forecast three cuts in 2024 (total 75bp) and four cuts in 2025 (total 100bp), bringing the DFR to 2.25% at the end of next year.

…and PEPP QT in July 2024: PEPP QT will begin in July 2024, with a full stop to reinvestments by the end of 2024. Should we see a continuation of the tight BTP-Bund spread regime also after the start of PEPP tapering, we would expect an earlier end to PEPP reinvestments, anticipated by one quarter in October 2024. On the operational framework, given that excess liquidity will only reach around €2.0 trillion in early 2026, we do not expect any significant decisions before 2026.

Risks: We see risks of a slower cutting cycle stemming from stickier inflation in the US, stronger world demand, or higher oil prices. On the other hand, downside surprises on the inflation front and significant weakening of wages could lead to a faster cutting cycle.

# Japan: The Year of Normalization

Takeshi Yamaguchi (81 3) 6836 5404

Masayuki Inui (81 3) 6836 5407

Exhibit 63: Japan's great escape from the lost decades
![](images/d6b1a5320604e55704043924010c6bb8df61d86e351e48004a8c988562b1ecc0.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 64: High correlation between nominal GDP and TOPIX
![](images/7fbcc30b8b36ec5b69db24755a1d44133c8152800258768ed4b244fac87ca9ed.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 65: We expect private capex to exceed its bubble-period peak in 1Q91
![](images/c1854bb0b80cb01e9eaa2dad7d2b5c812c5890de6081fb1a16b9d37227e23038.jpg)
Source: Cabinet Office, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts; Note: Shaded bars indicate official recession periods.

The Japanese economy has been shifting equilibria to an economy with positive nominal GDP growth with steady real growth and a sustained inflation and wage cycle. Domestic capex should exceed its bubble-era peak, partly due to gains from friendshoring/reshoring. We expect the BoJ to normalize rates policy further and reduce JGB purchases at a measured pace.

3%Y+ nominal GDP growth to continue: Japan has been shifting to a normal economy with positive nominal growth after decades of range-bound nominal GDP. We expect Japan’s nominal GDP growth to remain above 3%Y in 2024-25 on the back of sustained domestic inflation. The outlook is supported by wage growth, improved domestic demand/investment, and even upside risk to exports. On a quarter-on-quarter basis, we expect solid positive real GDP growth to resume from 2Q24 after temporary disruptions in domestic auto production, though near-term year-on-year growth should be affected by the base effect from a post-Covid surge in 1H23.

Private capex to exceed its bubble-era peak: We remain constructive on Japan’s laborsaving, productivity-enhancing capex given continued structural tightness in the labor market. An exit of low-productivity 'zombie' firms with deflationary business models should further support the trend. We also see tailwinds for domestic capex coming from reorganization of global supply chains, an upward shift in expected nominal domestic sales growth, low real interest rates with the yen remaining weak, pent-up demand for replacing old equipment, and the METI's new industrial policy targeting semiconductors. The Kyushu region, known　as Japan’s Silicon Island, will be a focal point for FDI inflows and investment by domestic manufacturers.

Gradual recovery in private consumption likely: Japan’s consumption recovery paused temporarily in 2H23, but service consumption remains relatively solid. The expected rise in electricity charges by the summer should delay the timing of real wage growth turning positive, but the overall recovery trend in consumption will likely remain on track on the back of the outlook of higher wage growth and a one-off income tax cut from June 2024. Inbound consumption by foreign tourists will likely remain solid.

2%Y+ Japan-style core inflation to continue until 1H25: We expect headline and Japanstyle core CPI (ex fresh food) to approach 3%Y in summer 2024 on the back of a rise in utility prices and yen depreciation before stabilizing slightly below 2%Y in 2H25. Japan’s underlying inflation should be supported by the virtuous cycle between wages and service inflation, though it will likely take time for Japan’s long-term inflation expectations to be well anchored at around 2%Y. Compared with our previous forecast, we now expect marginally higher inflation in 2H24 and 2025, reflecting the impact of a weaker yen.

BoJ to normalize policy at a measured pace: The BoJ should be able to confirm broad wage increases, including at SMEs, by the summer and implement the first hike to 0.25% in July 2024, followed by an additional hike to 0.50% in January 2025. We also expect the

Exhibit 66: Sustainable inflation has come into sight: Japan's various core CPI measures (%YoY)
![](images/2dc4190a17bf651b4d8e2c72215b6880a7892f287acdb3cb0fb9018093f857ce.jpg)
Source: MIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecast

BoJ to gradually reduce the pace of gross JGB purchases from October 2024. If inflation and wage growth turn out to be stronger than our forecast, we see a risk of the BoJ raising the policy rate again to 0.75% around July 2025, though this is not our base case. We would characterize the measured pace of rate hikes as 'less easing' rather than 'tightening,' as we estimate that Japan's nominal neutral rate is located at around 1% or marginally higher. We expect the BoJ to cite broad wage increases including among SMEs instead of yen depreciation when it raises the policy rate in July. The change in import prices on a yen basis has been much less than in 2022 so far. Our FX team expects the yen to gradually appreciate to around 146 against USD by end-2024, but the BoJ will likely respond if sustained yen depreciation, contrary to our house view, started to raise long-term inflation expectations and underlying inflation ahead.

Risks and potential catalysts: Upside risks include stronger-than-expected growth in China and the US accelerating Japan’s exports with the yen remaining weak, stronger consumption growth on the back of the wealth effect, and larger gains from friendshoring/reshoring. Downside risks include a delay in real wage growth turning positive adversely affecting sentiment and political uncertainty. Potential catalysts include households shifting away gradually from cash/deposits with higher inflation expectations and the government’s initiatives for a shift from savings to investment, improving labor market mobility especially among younger workers, a five-year plan for nurturing startups, and a potential snap election.

# UK: When the Rubber Meets the Road

Bruna Skarica

(44 20) 7425 9110

Exhibit 68: Government and net trade boost growth this year, with private consumption the key driver of the 2025 acceleration

![](images/f9f4d2689d6ef7bc4380f2a36462ace4e00dc1d923d9e99b51bd3b5c713b3477.jpg)
Source: ONS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 69: Labour market: Emerging slack and dissipating energy shock = wage growth normalization
![](images/28180323c9e0acaebb425bc439ebeae8bcf5eda61d83f5cef403a6bf6e79b651.jpg)
Source: ONS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts (grey shading)

Questions on the BoE’s record on inflation led former MPC member Tenreyro to say last year that “the exam [was] not over yet.” The next six months should provide concrete answers on the role of the (now dissipating) energy shock, or if structural shifts in recent years have left a permanently tighter economy. We have long advocated the former view. Core inflation should follow the energydriven decline in headline figures, with growth firming as the BoE removes restrictiveness, with Bank Rate at 4.5% at end-2024 and 3.25% at end-2025.

Growth | Accelerating – but the speed limit is low: After a sharp drop in 4Q23, GDP bounced back in 1Q24, but remains only modestly up on the year. Apart from some mechanical post-recession uplift, and a sharp drop in imports, we think that activity was helped by the front-loading of the impact of the anticipated rate cuts – with rate-sensitive sectors reporting a notable confidence uplift in recent months. We do see some modest payback in 2Q24 – a slowdown in growth to 0.2%Q – before the quarterly growth pace re-accelerates gradually towards 0.35%Q (1.4%Y), our estimate of UK potential growth.

Growth breakdown | Consumers: Much has been said about the annual growth rate in real wages turning positive in 2H23, with its peak, we think, materializing in 1Q24. That said, we think that some caution is advised before becoming overly optimistic on the consumer. For one, sequential quarterly improvements in real earnings have been a reality since early 2023, bringing about not a surge in real consumption growth, but rather a rise in the savings rate. In addition, (average) real wage growth does not equal (aggregate) real disposable income growth. The former rose by 2.2%Y in 2023, with the latter flat on the year. Indeed, we see real disposable income growth slowing to 1.0%Y in 2024 and 0.8%Y in 2025. Overall, consumption growth should improve this year – but gradually, from mildly negative figures in 1H24, towards \~1%Y growth in 4Q24.

Government, capex, and trade: Government spending growth should pick up somewhat too, as the economy leaves public sector strikes behind. We see a drag from public capex, however. Business capex should pick up on stronger aggregate demand and lower borrowing costs. Net trade remains a mild boost to growth this year, on ongoing solid demand growth among key trading partners. As domestic demand picks up next year, so do imports, and net trade turns to a mild drag.

Labor market | Catch-up time: Various labor market surveys suggest that the labor market is now broadly balanced. After a drop in hiring in 1Q24 (as per official data), we see moderate quarterly employment growth and an ongoing improvement in labor supply – helped by another 350-400k of net migrant arrivals this year. This should leave the jobless rate at still under 5% by the end of this year, hovering around the 5% threshold over 2025. With normalized inflation expectations, increased labor market slack, and a limited boost from minimum wage increases next year, regular wage growth falls towards 3.0% 3M/Y by 4Q25.

Exhibit 70: Inflation: Core will follow headline lower, we think

![](images/4bb20cccd04e7f76b305c8404fe7cbbbb01b1602aac8fb487f50b7a1e84aa860.jpg)
Source: ONS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts (grey shading)

Exhibit 71: BoE: Down...and in search of neutral
![](images/d92b310c335b7c285a95c4fdf1bc0a8eea2f628ab23465623040103b4698cc68.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts (purple line)

Inflation | Suddenly, then gradually: Momentum in inflation has looked very soft since last August. This, combined with sizeable base effects, should push headline inflation to below 2%Y in May and June. While the reversal in base effects implies some bounceback in headline figures in 2H24 (due to food and energy prices, in particular), we see inflation at around 2%Y over our forecast horizon. However, beyond mid-2024, core and headline disinflation should depend entirely on services prices. Softer pay growth next year, anchored inflation expectations, waning rental pressures, and less of an uplift from the indexation in administrative prices should all allow for services inflation to average 3%Y next year – leaving core at target over 2025. We see risks as broadly balanced.

BoE | Down...and into the unknown: As forecasts suggest inflation deeply below the 2%Y target over the medium term at unchanged Bank Rate, it is clear that cuts are coming. The removal of restrictiveness will likely be presented as a cautious and gradual endeavor, given still-elevated services inflation and wage growth. We see two rate cuts in the coming months – in June and August – with the BoE then pausing to assess the impact of its actions on the economy. We see a November cut thereafter. In 2025, as the BoE gains confidence that 2%Y core can be attained sustainably, we see it cutting towards 3.25% at successive meetings, from February next year. Active sales stop from next September, with passive QT and TFSME repayments driving balance sheet normalization.

Risks | Remixing policy? The UK's overall fiscal drag this year is 80bp, narrowing, on our estimates, to 40bp next year. Irrespective of the general election winner, we see some reversal of the recent tax cuts, in favor of topping up current spending plans. Hence, we anticipate a shift in underlying fiscal plans, but no extra layer of fiscal restrictiveness. That said, we see a bigger fiscal drag next year as the largest domestic downside risk to growth and our BoE forecasts. The global upside scenario sees stronger China-led growth, with the BoE stopping its cuts following the first 50bp of easing. The downside scenario sees US stagflation capping the initial BoE cuts at 50bp. Higher-for-longer rates eventually lead to a further deceleration in growth and a continuation of cuts next year.

Source: Destatis, Morgan Stanley Research Forecast

GDP growth resumes slowly in 2024 and accelerates in 2025 as exports and investment rebound. Structural headwinds could curtail medium-term growth.

Continued underperformance but growth gap narrows: After stagnation in 2023, we expect growth to resume slowly in 2024 and gain significant traction only in 2025 ( Exhibit 73 ). Hence, the growth gap vis-à-vis the euro area should remain sizeable this year and Germany exerts a significant downward drag on total euro area growth. The gap narrows thereafter and growth differentials across the largest euro area countries abate.

Consumption is the main near-term growth driver… Private consumption should increase in 2024 as strong nominal wage growth and falling inflation support real incomes ( Exhibit 75 ). Solid household savings – the savings rate has been hovering around 20% while it stands below 15% for the euro area – allow private consumption to increase further in 2025. Public consumption is turning positive after being a drag in 2023, when pandemic-related support measures were withdrawn. Together, private and public consumption provide sufficient support for positive albeit weak growth in 2024.

…while trade and investment should lift growth higher in 2025: Exports are showing the first signs of stabilization and contributed to the 1Q24 GDP expansion. However, export growth in 2024 should remain negative before a sharp rebound in 2025 as world trade picks up. In sync with the trade rebound, inventory growth should fall and weigh negatively on GDP. We forecast negative investment growth this year as higher interest rates continue to feed through to the economy. The propagation is likely to bottom out during 1H24 and monetary policy easing accelerates investment growth in 2025.

Structural headwinds restrict the speed limit: In the medium term, Germany faces growth challenges from structural headwinds (see our deep dive): elevated energy prices, stagnant world trade, including competition in core export industries, as well as adverse demographics that weigh on labor supply. This is most directly felt in the manufacturing sector, where weak momentum predates the pandemic ( Exhibit 74 ). Falling energy prices and higher demand for German manufacturing goods should bring some recovery, but the structural challenges risk lower growth for longer. On our estimates, potential GDP could decline by 2.7-3.2%, and potential growth could fall by 0.4pp by the end of the decade.

Restrictive fiscal policy as the debt brake debate continues: Reforms could address weaker structural growth, and additional fiscal spending would also help. Yet, neither seems imminent. The government is expected to present policy proposals in June, but an agreement, implementation, and resulting growth impact will take time. The 2025 budget negotiations might again be challenging, following last year's Constitutional Court ruling on the debt brake. The debate around the debt brake remains vivid, but a reform is unlikely before the next elections as it lacks the required majority. Alas, consolidation should continue and the primary deficit could decline from 1.6% in 2023 to 0.5% in 2025.

Source: Insee, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect GDP growth to be stable in 2024 and pick up a touch in 2025. France faces two main challenges: low productivity gains and a high deficit. Risks to growth are probably tilted to the downside in case of a larger fiscal consolidation in 2025.

Low volatility, in both directions: France, as usual, is proving to be a less volatile economy than the rest of the euro area. It experienced significantly higher growth in 2023 when Europe stalled. But, as the euro area gains momentum, this gap is likely to narrow. So, we think France will be experiencing growth just below the euro area average in 2025 and see significant hurdles for a more favorable outlook.

Not many tailwinds: With again positive real income gains, consumption should remain robust. But the elevated saving rate is unlikely to drop, as we think it reflects a shift in the composition of aggregate income to higher incomes. As already observed since 2019, France's exports are also likely to underperform other countries and thus benefit less from the expected global recovery. And, of course, fiscal policy should start to be a drag.

Two key challenges... France faces two structural challenges that are likely to shape the outlook. The first relates to productivity, which has fallen by 4pp since 2019 ( Exhibit 76 ). The second is fiscal, with France printing a deficit of 5.5% of GDP in 2023 ( Exhibit 77 ).

...falling productivity... On the surface, the French labor market looks like a success story: the unemployment rate is at 7.5% in 4Q23, 1pp lower than in 2019, while the participation rate is at the highest since this has been measured by Insee (1975). Almost 1.3 million jobs have been added since 2019, of which very few are in the public sector. But the flip side has been the observed drop in productivity. It can be only partially explained by labor market policies (subsidized apprenticeships) and labor hoarding (Banque de France explains 60% of the drop with those two factors). If persistent, this is a clear risk to French productivity gains and potential growth in the long run.

...and a high fiscal deficit: In 2023, the French fiscal deficit reached 5.5% of GDP, increasing meaningfully from 4.8% in 2022. This is the consequence of significant tax cuts implemented since 2021 while spending (as a percentage of GDP) remains higher than pre-Covid. From there, the direction of travel is clear: France is very likely to face an Excessive Deficit Procedure (EDP) this summer and will be required to reduce its deficit towards 3%. The French government intends to bring it back to 5.1% of GDP in 2024 and 4.1% in 2025, before getting below 3% in 2027. We think that the target for 2024 is plausible, given announced spending cuts. But, as it stands, 2025 will require additional spending cuts or tax increases which are not yet documented. More fundamentally, to get its debt path on a sustainable track, France needs to stop posting primary deficits like it has done since 2008. Fiscal consolidation is likely to be the main risk to growth.

Source: ISTAT, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

A correction in construction activity is likely, with the end of the Superbonus.
Fiscal deficits remain too high, and debt/GDP is on a rising path to 2025.

Supergrowth thanks to the Superbonus? The Italian economy has outperformed the euro area in the past two years. While post-pandemic tailwinds supporting the services sector can explain part of it, one of the main reasons behind the relative strength of the Italian economy has been the Superbonus. From the implementation of this tax credit scheme in 2020 until now, the construction portion of industrial production is up 60%; other major euro area economies are flat. Construction activity has had material spillovers onto the industrial sector too (the only category within Italian industrial production to have seen positive growth versus the end of 2019 is investment goods). The government has ended the Superbonus with one last decree in March 2024. However, the take-up of the scheme extended to the first three months of 2024, and that will likely continue to support construction activity throughout the year, together with the Superbonus works that have started in late 2023 but are not yet completed. From the end of 2024 onwards, we expect a normalization in the flow of construction activity in Italy, only in part balanced out by the positive effects of the recovery fund. We see negative growth (- 0.1%Y) in construction in 2025. On the back of this dynamic, we revise our 2025 GDP forecast down by 30bp to 0.7%Y (consensus: 1.1%Y). We see risks skewed towards a faster correction in construction activity, in particular if the RRF implementation delays continue.

Good news from the labor market: Participation and the employment rate are at record levels as of the start of 2024. Employment growth has been strong (3.2% since end-2019), but driven by only a few selected pockets. Almost half of that growth is concentrated in professional, science, tech and administration, a third in construction (Superbonus again), and a fifth comes from the public sector. With the envisaged slowdown, employment growth should decline significantly. Wage growth has lagged in Italy, both in nominal and real terms. In this context, Italian households have reduced their saving rate well below pre-pandemic levels, to consume a similar basket in real terms as before the inflation shock. We think that this is only temporary, and as real wage growth picks up throughout 2024, the saving rate should also return to more normal levels.

Fiscal | It's not what it looks like: The impact of the Superbonus on public finances is as yet to show up in full. The measure is accounted in the deficit of the year in which the tax credit is approved, but the impact on debt and the borrowing requirement takes place when the lack of revenues from the tax credits materializes for the government. As a result, we see debt/GDP on a rising path, mostly caused by an adverse stock-flow adjustment on the back of the Superbonus. We expect a primary deficit in both 2024 and 2025. While Italy will likely end up in an EDP, we think that the actual implications are limited: the government's fiscal plan should meet EC requirements under the EDP, and in case of compliance with SGP rules, Italy would remain eligible under the ECB's TPI.

Private consumption should start to pick up in 2025, while migration can continue to provide support to the labour market.

Spain is on a streak… 1Q24 GDP growth surprised meaningfully to the upside. It came in at 0.7%Q versus our expectation at 0.4%Q. INE’s report shows four interesting facts: 1) Private consumption was relatively muted (at 0.3%Q) versus an overall stronger GDP print; 2) Government consumption declined significantly (at -1.0%Q), which could reflect some intended fiscal consolidation or be a by-product of the 2024 budget bill not having been passed (or both); 3) The contribution from fixed capital investment was large and positive, in part boosted by construction; and 4) Services exports were particularly strong and, while we expect Spain to continue to perform well, we also think that some of this might be related to a temporary boost from an early Easter. Taking stock of 1Q24, we have updated our forecast. We expect the country to continue outperforming the euro area in the near term. We see 2024 GDP growth at 2.0%Y, pushed considerably by carryover effects, while 2025 should be at 1.9%Y, supported by more consumption growth.

…due to its labor market catching up... The Spanish labor market has shown itself to be especially dynamic. Its economy relies on labor-intensive services, representing a sizeable proportion of its labor market. And these sectors were particularly affected by the pandemic, which led to higher job losses relative to its euro area counterparts. From 4Q19 to 2Q20, employment dropped by 8pp versus 3pp for the euro area. This was clear in the trade, tourism, food, and recreation sectors, which contributed 6pp to the initial decline in 2Q20. Since then, the Spanish labor market has recovered its losses, but cumulative gains in employment growth are not materially above the euro area. More importantly, around half of employment gains were driven by the public sector, which is more likely to be affected by fiscal consolidation in the future ( Exhibit 82 ).

…and recent migration flows: The main question from here is whether employment will continue to grow at a similar pace. And the answer lies, in part, with migration. In 2022, net migration reached 700,000 people and it appears that 2023 was comparable. Many foreigners have entered the labour market by taking advantage of a reform introduced in August 2022, which brought more flexible migration laws for non-EU citizens. In less than two years, it has allowed for the foreign population in the country to grow by around 2pp. In turn, the working age population has responded by growing at a faster pace ( Exhibit 83 ), and this ultimately allowed for the labor force and employment to follow a similar trajectory. Overall, while these policies are in place, net migration should remain elevated, which is likely to fuel employment.
