Source: Statistics Norway, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect mainland GDP growth at 0.8%Y in 2024 and to pick up further in 2025 to 1.5%Y. The main inflationary challenge for Norway stems from high real wage growth in 2024 and the weak krone. We see Norges Bank cutting in September, with risks tilted toward a later start.

Staying strong: The Norwegian economy proved resilient this cycle and should continue to do so. Norway's households will be in an even better position this year due to higher real wage gains than initially expected. Private consumption should recover further in 2025 as the anticipated rate cuts free up more disposable income on the back of lower interest rate expenses. Strong oil and gas investments from last year should fuel offshore support sectors further this year and are likely to increase even more. Looking ahead, while we do not expect a boost from construction for investment this year, higher house prices should start to fuel improved sentiment in the construction sector going into 2025. Mainland exports are likely to be supported by the weak currency and improved global trade.

Fiscal policy to remain expansionary: The revised budget this spring brought an additional NOK 36.2 billion of spending. The focus is primarily on defense, security, and policing. NATO's target will already be met this year. Taking into account the increased value of the fund, the withdrawal from the pension fund remained 2.7%.

Sticky inflation: We expect core inflation to be sticky in 1H24. Core goods have driven the disinflation process, but services remain strong and should stay elevated. The list of inflationary pressures in the pipeline remains long: wage negotiations, an increase in business costs, and pass-through of a weaker krone. This underpins our call for persistent inflation and a cautious Norges Bank.

Strong wage growth: Wage growth this year should be higher than expected. We do not expect a significant wage drift, and hence see wage growth coming in at 5.2%Y, in line with the central framework agreement. Given the challenging backdrop and low profitability in some sectors (e.g., manufacturing and construction), we can expect a higher-than anticipated rise in business costs and hence rising inflationary pressures.

Cut in September: The policy rate is likely to be maintained at 4.5% for "some time ahead." Norges Bank is likely to be more cautious due to this year's wage negotiations. On the back of a relatively resilient growth outlook and labor market, it can maintain restrictive monetary policy for a bit longer than its DM peers. We see Norges Bank cutting for the first time by 25bp in September 2024. We think that the cutting cycle will be gradual, and hence only see Norges Bank cutting at projection meetings. On balance we see cuts of 50bp in 2024 and 100bp in 2025. The balance of risks is tilted toward a later start on the back of stronger inflationary pressures arising from higher-than-expected wage growth in 2024, and a weaker krone.

The Riksbank cut policy rates in May to 3.75%, ahead of the Fed and the ECB.
We expect lackluster growth in 2024 at -0.1%Y and recovery in 2025 to 1.3%Y.
Inflation is converging to the target, but the weak SEK poses a challenge.

Growth finally looking up: Forward-looking indicators in Sweden finally show a slightly better picture. Even though the Swedish consumer remains weak due to a challenging labor market and low wage growth in 2024, positive sentiment is finally returning. The Riksbank introduced a first cut, and lower mortgage rates are gradually easing the pain inflicted by high interest rate expenses. Yet, private consumption is likely to pick up only in 2H24 and mainly in 2025. On the other hand, government spending should be the backbone of Swedish growth throughout 2024 and 2025. The Spring Budget Bill introduced SEK 16.8 billion of additional spending. The Bill also signaled larger investments in infrastructure for 2025, together with various tax cuts. Moving away from domestic factors, we anticipate growth to pick up in 2Q24 on the back of improved external demand. The more supportive outlook for key trade partners like the euro area and the US should also support Swedish growth going forward.

Inflation outlook, focus on SEK: Inflation prints and momentum have been encouraging in Sweden, with the risk of undershooting the target in some months now present too. However, services remain quite persistent and the currency is weak. The May cut has increased risks of further SEK weakening. A weak krona is a concern as long as it is passed through to consumers. Hence, the strengthening consumer and increasing demand in the latter part of the year pose an upside risk.

On hold until September: Domestic economic concerns prevailed over the external factors and the weaker currency at the May meeting. We think that the Riksbank will cut again only in September, after the ECB has cut twice and the Fed has kicked off its cutting cycle. Overall, we expect two more cuts in 2H24 at the projection meetings, in line with the Riksbank's guidance.

Risks around the forecast: The balance of risks has tilted toward the Riksbank introducing fewer cuts than we have priced in and not delivering on its projected policy rate path (50bp more in 2024 and 100bp in 2025). We think that there are still substantial upside risks to the inflation outlook – krona weakness, higher oil prices, and elevated services inflation. Even though they have not led the Board to wait and see until June to make the first cut against a full assessment of new projections, we think that a materialization of these risks could slow down the cutting path going forward. In a small, open economy, significant weight is put on FX considerations, interest rate expectations abroad, and geopolitical developments.

Source: Statistics Canada, Bank of Canada, IMF, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Faster population growth and lower interest rates in 2H24 put a floor under the slowdown in economic activity this year. After rising 1.1%Y in 2023, GDP slows a bit to 0.9%Y in 2024 before rebounding to 1.8%Y in 2025 as monetary easing stimulates the economy.

Economy: Summer rate cuts support growth in 2H24 through next year. The pick-up in growth primarily reflects stronger consumption, a revival in residential investment, less weakness in non-residential business investment, and export growth driven by robust US demand. In 2025, domestic activity rebounds further as interest rates fall further. Strong population growth from immigration continues to provide a floor for underlying aggregate demand in 2024 but slows in 2025 as the federal government lowers its target for nonpermanent residents. Job gains soften in 2024 and remain soft until picking up in 2H25. From 5.4% in 2023, the unemployment rate increases in 2024 before edging down in 2025, as the rebound in economic activity increases demand and supply for labor. Capex investment picks up in 2H24 and into 2025 due to easing financial conditions, lower rates, and stronger economic growth.

Monetary policy: After a faster-than-expected deceleration in inflation in 1Q24, coupled with the BoC expressing comfort on diverging its monetary policy with the Fed, we continue to expect four 25bp cuts. Given that the macro outlook hasn’t really changed, but now coupled with better-than-expected progress on inflation, the BoC cuts by an additional 150bp by September 2025 after the Fed has finished. The BoC continues to reduce the size of its balance sheet through mid-2025.

Inflation: Headline inflation continues to fall through our forecast period as easing supply chain conditions flow through, particularly in 2H24 and into 2025. Core goods prices decline broadly due to falling import prices, past declines in energy prices flowing through, and tepid demand growth. Services ex shelter prices moderate as the labor market eases and wage growth slows. Shelter costs benefit from policy easing (i.e., lower mortgage rates), though strong demand spurred by better affordability limits downside in shelter inflation.

Risks: Downside risks include the BoC being more cautious than expected on inflation and waiting to cut until September. Concurrently, restrictive monetary policy weighs more heavily on the economy. The economy enters a recession in 2Q24-3Q24. A delayed recognition leads the BoC to deliver a second 25bp cut in December and an abrupt end to QT. The BoC goes on to deliver aggressive policy cuts of 225bp in 2025 to revitalize economic growth. Upside risks include a stronger Chinese and global economy that leads to stronger export growth for Canada. Notably, exports of raw materials benefit from stronger Chinese demand for commodities, while exports to the US accelerate. The pace of disinflation in goods prices slows, while energy prices push headline inflation higher. The BoC delays the start of cuts until early 2025, cutting by 100bp in 2025.

Source: ABS, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 95: Key forecast summary
<table><tr><td>Australia Forecast Summary</td><td>2022</td><td></td><td>2023 2024E 2025E</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Real GDP (%Y)</td><td>3.8</td><td>2.1</td><td>1.6</td><td>2.4</td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption Gross fixed investment</td><td>6.6</td><td>1.1 5.5</td><td>0.7</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption</td><td>2.1 5.0</td><td>1.7</td><td>2.4 2.4</td><td>2.4 1.7</td></tr><tr><td>Contribution to GDP (pp)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Final domestic demand</td><td>5.2</td><td>1.2</td><td>1.6</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Net exports</td><td>-1.8</td><td>1.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories CPI (%Y)</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.9</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Core CPI (%Y)</td><td>6.6 5.4</td><td>5.6 5.4</td><td>3.4 3.6</td><td>3.0 3.0</td></tr><tr><td>Policy rate (EOP, %)</td><td>3.10</td><td>4.35</td><td>4.35</td><td>3.60</td></tr><tr><td>Unemp. rate (% labor force)</td><td>3.7</td><td>3.7</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.6</td></tr><tr><td>Labor force participation rate (%)</td><td>66.4</td><td>66.6</td><td>66.7</td><td>66.5</td></tr><tr><td>General govt. balance (% GDP)</td><td>-1.4</td><td>0.9</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Gross govt. debt (% GDP)</td><td>38.4</td><td>34.7</td><td>35.8</td><td>36.3</td></tr><tr><td>Current account balance (% GDP)</td><td>1.1</td><td>0.9</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-1.2</td></tr></table>

Source: ABS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect fiscal stimulus to be supportive through 2H24 and keep inflation above target, with wages and rents remaining particularly sticky. The RBA remains on hold until early 2025, when labor market weakness is more evident and allows for a cut.

Demand improvement from current weakness: We expect GDP growth to rise sequentially through 2024 but remain below trend. Income tax cuts due mid-year are a key driver, boosting household incomes by 1.3% and seeing consumer spending rise. We also expect investment to improve, although more gradually – residential construction should stay weak all year but some improvement in business investment is expected – with both mining and non-mining contributing. Migration will likely remain a key tailwind to underlying demand – although we expect the run-rate to slow from \~550k in 2023 to \~330k in 2024 – contributing \~1.2pp to population growth.

Labor softening but wage inertia: The labor market remains the key variable for the economic outlook, particularly over 2H24. Whether increases in household incomes are spent is consequential. We think that the current trend of gradual softening is likely to continue – as suggested by most forward-looking labor demand indicators. We expect the unemployment rate to rise through the course of both 2024 and 2025. Despite this, we expect wage growth to ease only gradually – collective agreements (which make up \~40% of the workforce) continue to reset higher and should remain a sizeable wage tailwind for the next 18 months.

Inflation's slow progress: After a sequential acceleration in most inflation measures over 1Q24, we expect a disinflationary path to continue through 2024 – goods should continue to drive most of this, with services sticky given still-tight labour markets, wage-setting inertia, and very strong rental inflation. We expect core inflation to move back into the RBA's 2-3%Y target band later in 2025, as the labor market cools sufficiently to see broader progress on services inflation.

RBA holding more hawkishly: After stronger-than-expected data in 1Q24, we think that near-term risks for the RBA are tilted toward tightening over easing with more hawkish language. The RBA has chosen to set policy less restrictive than other central banks (and accept a slower inflation decline), but this is challenged by: 1) Greater-than-expected labor market resilience; 2) Fiscal policy turning more stimulatory; and 3) Expectations of later and less policy easing from other central banks. Ultimately, though, we think that the bar for the RBA to restart tightening remains high and would require a more persistent lift in inflation, which we don’t expect. We continue to see the first cut in February 2025, with two further cuts through the year to leave the cash rate at 3.6%.

Risks: The key domestic risks revolve around the labor market. Weaker-than-expected near-term trends would blunt tax cuts, leading to weaker growth and inflation, while signs of re-tightening would be the key catalyst for the RBA to hike rates.

# China: Steady Yet Subdued

Robin Xing

(852) 2848 6511

Exhibit 96: Nominal GDP growth has bottomed out
![](images/71b815e4dfc3d7c42f4747bec1bb829bceced93cf068ac82b0166fd4f56e535e.jpg)
Source: NBS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 97: From deflation to lowflation
![](images/72c42cdd94d685809e319222560112e740c82c6ffd253263d8bf151b6483f660.jpg)
Source: NBS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

China Economics Team

Real GDP growth has stabilized on exports and manufacturing capex, but we think that nominal GDP growth will remain below consensus at a subdued level (4.5%Y in 2024 and 4.9%Y in 2025), as the current policy approach of pushing investment in manufacturing rather than boosting consumption means persistent deflationary pressure.

Real GDP growth is stabilizing on exports and manufacturing capex: We expect real GDP growth to reach 4.8%Y in 2024 with a strong entry of 5.3%Y in 1Q24. Within this, export volume growth will likely remain robust at 8%Y in 2024 (versus 2%Y in 2023 and 10%Y in 1Q24), benefiting from the global trade recovery. Domestically, manufacturing and green capex should remain strong, outweighing continued softness in construction activities – this means a modest acceleration in growth of fixed capital formation to 4.0%Y (versus 3.8%Y in 2023). However, private consumption growth will likely slow to 4.8%Y (versus a 5.1% 2Y CAGR in 2022-23) amid fading revenge spending and a soft job market. For 2025, we assume moderating manufacturing investment and rising trade tensions, as the ongoing supply-driven growth may be constrained by overcapacity and geopolitical pushback. These headwinds could be cushioned by a gradual stabilization in the housing market. We see real GDP growth moderating to 4.5%Y in 2025.

Yet nominal GDP growth would remain subpar: As the current policy approach of pushing investment in manufacturing would mean persistent deflationary pressure, we remain firmly below consensus on nominal GDP growth. We expect the GDP deflator (the most comprehensive inflation gauge) to remain subdued at -0.3% for 2024 (-0.5% in 2023) and rebound modestly to 0.4% in 2025. Nominal GDP growth would thus average at a subpar <5%Y over our forecast horizon. Within underlying details, we see core CPI remaining largely stable at 0.7%Y in 2024 and 0.8%Y in 2025: services prices will likely remain resilient considering relatively tight supply amid population ageing offsetting subdued core goods prices with pass-through from PPI. We expect PPI to remain in deflation up to 1Q25, only to exit deflation from 2Q25 as manufacturing capex moderates while lower prices will likely have squeezed out some inefficient supply.

The housing market could stabilise gradually in 2025 amid support measures: The housing downturn is entering its fourth year, with housing sales volume and new starts having slumped by 40% and 70%, respectively, while housing prices are down by 25-30% versus the peak in early 2021. We expect further relaxation of transaction restrictions and modest central government funding to help to "digest housing inventory" (nationwide implementation likely from 2025). These should facilitate faster market clearing, support liquidity among developers, restore home buyer confidence, and (with a lag) improve housing investment. We think that the housing sector will likely pose a smaller drag on the economy in 2025.

Exhibit 98: Housing market to stabilize gradually in 2025, with risks still skewed to the downside
![](images/59e8cad7037bbb9ba86a28d79e5104714995a4574abfa4d750b688dd47a96dea.jpg)
Source: NBS, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 99: Mild fiscal expansion
![](images/eff2cdf5ace6afefca5bdd3fa149021630184649215ce0d646a19163e210d6b9.jpg)
Source: Wind, CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Modest fiscal easing continues: We expect the augmented fiscal deficit to widen slightly by 0.5pp of GDP this year (in line with the NPC budget) as real GDP growth of >5%Y in 1H24 would have minimized the likelihood of mid-year budget expansion. Policymakers would focus on implementing the manufacturing equipment upgrade scheme and the (less sizeable) "cash for clunkers" program for autos. We expect another 0.5pp expansion in 2025 amid exports headwinds. Meanwhile, we expect modest monetary easing in view of elevated real interest rates. We see a 25-50bp RRR cut to accommodate acceleration in government bond issuance in the coming months; a Fed pivot in September would create room for a total 20bp of policy rate cuts cumulatively for the remainder of 2024. In 2025, we see the PBOC cutting the 7-day OMO rate to 1.45% (1.6% in 2024) and 1Y MLF rate to 2.0% (versus 2.3% in 2024), slightly asymmetric to strike a balance between managing capital market leverage and promoting a lower lending rate (LPR is priced based on MLF).

Risks: The downside incorporates an entrenched debt-deflation loop amid worsening housing/LGFV deleveraging, while exports slow sharply on rising trade barriers. As weaker final demand exacerbates price pressures, firms would cut wage growth and households may start to deleverage. In this case, real GDP growth would slow to 4.0%Y in 2024 and 3.2%Y in 2025, while the GDP deflator may slip to -1.2% and -2.0%, respectively. On the upside, we assume a more decisive reflation and faster rebalancing toward consumption facilitated by more active fiscal easing, lifting real GDP growth to 5.3%Y in 2024 and 5.5%Y in 2025, with a turnaround in the GDP deflator to 0.5% and 2.5%, respectively. Nominal GDP thus returns to desirable levels of 6-7%Y.

Exhibit 100:Key forecast summary

# India: Strength and Stability in the Economy

Upasana Chachra

(91 22) 618 2246

Exhibit 101:High-frequency indicators suggest continued strength

![](images/18e51c220b3d8d5bc909ff24c532bb5efa6e87f97346b44405a9208bf4efdbe6.jpg)
Source: CEIC, GSTN, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 102:We expect GDP growth to remain resilient

![](images/ec3165877a937d1b4777a9a114962233c3438517feb174336dbdf181893e07aa.jpg)
Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts
Exhibit 103:Trend in headline and core CPI, %Y

![](images/a24e9063b0049ac784e86f270eb78be01e0120cdd3538cd0310808fbd70416dc.jpg)
Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research

Bani Gambhir

(91 22) 618 3027

Robust domestic demand keeps broad-based growth at or above 6.5%Y over the next two years. We expect the cycle to run longer, driven by productivityenhancing growth keeping macro stability risks at bay. Risks emerge from the general election outcome or cyclical risks from external factors.

Growth – strength to continue: Incoming growth data remain steadfast, with PMIs remaining above 50 since August 2021, credit growth at a robust \~16% and GST collections reaching an all-time high in April, Indeed, we expect growth to be broad-based as rural demand picks up, alongside urban consumption and private capex improving, in addition to public capex. In our view, GDP growth is likely to track at 6.8%Y in 2024 and 6.5%Y in 2025. We enumerate sector-wise trends below.

Consumption – recovery to broaden with improvement in rural demand: Consumption growth has been improving. but it has lagged capex growth in this cycle. We expect private consumption growth to recover further and the gap between rural and urban demand to narrow, driven by: 1) A moderation in inflation, which improves purchasing power; 2) Improving terms of trade for the rural sector; 3) A steady trend in net household financial savings; and 4) Improvement in the employment outlook.

Capex – private capex recovery to gather momentum: This cycle, like the 2003-07 one, has been marked by a pick-up in capex, with public capex spending taking the lead. In our view, the nascent signs of capex revival will become more broad-based and sustain the uptrend, driven by: 1) Strength in private sector balance sheets, both corporate and financial sector; 2) Improving end demand, reflected in rising capacity utilization; 3) Structural policy reforms which have improved the environment for private businesses; and 4) Support from diversification of global supply chains.

Exports – sequential trend likely to improve: Goods and services exports account for \~22% of GDP, and the trend in exports tends to influence domestic production and capex cycles. We note that exports have recovered from their trough in mid-2023. With global growth expected to largely remain steady, we expect the export trend to stabilize and not be a further drag on growth. Furthermore, over the medium term, we estimate an increase in export market share as India benefits from supply chain diversification and policy measures supporting manufacturing.

Inflation – moderating, but staying above the 4%Y target: Headline inflation softened to 5.4%Y in F2024, in line with expectations, versus 6.7%Y in F2023. Food, which has a disproportionately large weight of 45.86% in the CPI basket, remains sticky and continues to impart volatility to the overall headline print. Core CPI, on the other hand, is tracking below the 4%Y mark, an all-time low, led by broad-based softening across both core goods and core services. In our base case, we anticipate supply-side normalization to get established further and commodity price pressures to remain under control. We anticipate

Exhibit 104:We expect policy rates to remain steady over our forecast horizon

![](images/fe5800a8ec863414b82796e380278f98ed619da5a0b84ee4f68b8302cac1a8cf.jpg)
Source: CEIC, RBI, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

a normal south-west monsoon, which lends support to food inflation trends. We expect headline inflation to average 4.5-4.6%Y in 2024-25, while core is likely to average lower in 2024, and inch up closer to the headline average in 2025, driven by services inflation.

Current account deficit – range-bound trend with strength in the external balance sheet: In our base case, the current account deficit is likely to remain range-bound at 1- 1.5% of GDP in 2024-25, supported by a likely steady trend in the merchandise trade deficit and improving trend in the services trade surplus. As such, India's external balance sheet position remains strong, reflected in rising FX reserves, improving import cover and stable external debt/GDP. Furthermore, the inclusion of India in the GBI-EM index from June 2024 will likely trigger capital flows and bolster the balance of payments further.

Monetary policy – steady trend in rates reflecting higher neutral real rates: We expect the RBI to keep rates at 6.5% in 2024-25, led by the confluence of global and domestic factors. On the global front, a shallower rate easing cycle by the Fed and a strong dollar, coupled with the continued strength in growth, driven by capex and productivity, on the domestic front, warrant the RBI staying put. As such, in our view, real rates will average 200bp, similar to the average of 190bp during 2003-07.

Fiscal policy – consolidation to gather pace: On the fiscal side, we expect momentum in fiscal consolidation to pick up, as we see the consolidated deficit narrowing to 8.2% of GDP in 2024 from 9.1% in 2023, driven by an increase in tax revenues and rationalization of revenue spending, even as capex spending remains robust.

Risks – more from cyclical and global factors: The key near-term risk would be a surprise general election outcome in June. Other upside risks emanate from an improvement in external demand feeding into goods exports, while also leading to a quicker recovery in private capex with positive spillovers for consumption as well. In contrast, downside risks stem from a weaker-than-expected trend in domestic and external demand, and adverse geopolitical conditions, which could weigh on corporate confidence and push back the capex recovery. Furthermore, changes in global commodity prices and/or food prices can pose idiosyncratic risks impacting growth and inflation adversely.

# Indonesia: Navigating Through Uncertainties

Derrick Kam

(65) 6834 8272

![](images/2dc1f7af5ec7a4e22f9349c9d84afcede9b50541f3617cdac5bbc3ca40c0746d.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: Numbers in brackets indicate the share in overall loans.
Exhibit 107:Investment cycle – still room to rise

![](images/d29af30df888e2f50682698e4188cb15460baaa44a2deb86833918a9e7105e83.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research

Growth is robust and macro stability is in check, suggesting that the economy has navigated through the Fed and fiscal policy outlook uncertainties so far. This uncertainty should dispel, eventually strengthening capex growth.

Growth is robust: The Indonesian economy has managed to navigate a series of near-term headwinds and has continued to post robust growth. Headline GDP growth rose to 5.1%Y in 1Q24 from 4.9%Y in 3Q23. PMIs are holding up at elevated levels relative to history. Credit growth has also improved in 2024 so far. There are a number of reasons underpinning the robust growth performance. First, inflation has been less of a challenge and this has helped to preserve consumer purchasing power. Second, there were still benefits to be reaped from trailing improvement in the terms of trade. Third, even though policy rates have been hiked by 275bp, the transmission to lending rates has been far more muted. What is encouraging about this robust growth backdrop is that the economy has had to navigate a challenging external backdrop of a strong US dollar amid a higher-forlonger rate environment.

Dispelling the clouds of uncertainty: In the near term, some uncertainties remain. Following the February 2024 presidential elections, investors have been concerned about the future conduct of fiscal policy. In particular, the focus is around the incoming administration's campaign pledge to provide free lunches to school children, potentially at a huge fiscal cost. Externally, the US dollar environment staying stronger for longer should continue to pose headwinds. Both these factors may keep market-based interest rates high in the near term, potentially weighing on the capex recovery. However, we do expect clarity over fiscal policy to emerge and our base case is that the incoming administration will continue to prioritize a prudent approach in the conduct of fiscal policy. In this regard, we would be watching for announcements on cabinet appointments, which will likely be unveiled before the new administration takes office in October 2024. As we move into 2025, we expect that the start to the Fed rate cut cycle and our strategists' forecasts of the US dollar weakening will pave the way for BI to cut rates from early 2025 onwards.

Capex momentum is key to watch: Post-pandemic, the economic recovery has been led by a boom in commodity exports, followed by a rise in consumption activity. As the recovery gains further traction, we expect growth to be increasingly driven forward by the capex cycle. Encouragingly, capex growth had picked up over 2H23, though the momentum did moderate at the margin in 1Q24. Taking a step back, the investment/GDP ratio in Indonesia is at 29%, which is somewhat below the pre-Covid level of around 32%. At the current stage of the recovery, a pick-up in capex is needed for employment creation in a way that would create a self-sustaining virtuous growth cycle. As we discussed earlier, the dispelling of the uncertainties should be key to unlocking stronger capex growth, which would help to lift overall growth momentum.

Macro stability to stay in check: Headline CPI inflation has been within the central bank's target range for the past 11 months. At the same time, while softer commodity prices have meant that the current account balance has moved from a position of surplus to deficit in the previous three quarters, it has remained below 1% of GDP, well within the comfort zone, we think. One key anchor has been that the non-commodity trade balance has not widened by as much as compared to previous cycles and this has mitigated the downdraft on the overall current account balance. At the moment, real rates in Indonesia are high relative to its history and so they should act to keep key macro stability indicators within the central bank's comfort zone.

Rate cuts can begin from early 2025: BI surprised the market by hiking last October and as recently as April as it sought to fight off currency depreciation headwinds. At its latest meeting, BI dropped its previous guidance that it could consider and discuss rate cuts in 2H24. On the flipside, the governor has said in a post-meeting media interview that the latest rate hike has helped to attract capital inflows and the stabilization of the currency has meant that BI will likely not need to hike rates again. With this backdrop in mind, we expect the rate cut cycle to begin in 1Q25, but we still expect a total of 100bp by 3Q25 given the benign inflation dynamics. We also expect that the pace of rate cuts will be more distributed and so rate cuts will happen once every other month.

Risks – all about policy: Risks to growth stem mainly from the monetary and fiscal policy outlook. On the monetary policy front, if US data remain strong and markets price out Fed rate cuts, it will be the case that BI may have to stay on hold for longer than we expect or even be prompted to hike again. This in turn would weigh on corporate confidence and capex momentum. On fiscal policy, we believe that the prudent conduct of fiscal policy – in keeping the fiscal deficit target below 3% of GDP – has been one of the key structural anchors in keeping inflation and cost of capital lower. A continued lack of clarity on this front or a disruptive shift towards pursuing expansionary fiscal policies would raise significant concerns among market participants, potentially raising market interest rates, which will therefore raise investor concerns and may trigger bond outflows and lead to higher market-based interest rates, in turn weighing again on capex.

# Philippines: Challenged by Twin Deficits

Derrick Kam

(65) 6834 8272

![](images/28352a4f8a3aedb3821e6715727fe7007dfe0918e961a65ad470be7983a51bbe.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 110:Sharp rise in public debt postpandemic
![](images/675278c6d8c326594f5808b010473b84508d0799ce4c29c0c3db484d6c1784d0.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 111:Key forecast summary
<table><tr><td>Philippines Forecast Summary</td><td>2022</td><td>2023 2024E 2025E</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Real GDP (%Y)</td><td>7.6</td><td>5.6</td><td>5.4</td><td>5.1</td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption Gross fixed investment</td><td>8.4 9.8</td><td>5.6 8.4</td><td>5.1 4.1</td><td>5.1 5.0</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption</td><td>4.8</td><td>1.2</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.0</td></tr><tr><td>Contribution to GDP (pp) Final domestic demand</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Net exports</td><td>9.0</td><td>6.0</td><td>5.1</td><td>5.3</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>-2.2</td><td>0.0</td><td>-1.0</td><td>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td>CPI (%Y)</td><td>0.8 5.8</td><td>-0.5 6.0</td><td>1.2 4.3</td><td>0.4 3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Core CPI (%Y)</td><td>5.1</td><td>7.1</td><td>3.9</td><td>3.5</td></tr><tr><td>Policy rate (EOP, %) General govt. balance (% GDP)</td><td>5.50</td><td>6.50</td><td>6.50</td><td>5.50</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>-5.5</td><td>-5.0</td><td>-4.5</td><td>-4.3</td></tr><tr><td>Gross govt. debt (% GDP)</td><td>57.4</td><td>57.5</td><td>56.8</td><td>56.9</td></tr><tr><td>Current account balance (% GDP)</td><td>-4.7</td><td>-2.7</td><td>-3.2</td><td>-2.8</td></tr></table>

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecast

We expect GDP growth to average just 5.4%Y over 2024-25, a full percentage point lower than the pre-Covid decade average of 6.4%Y. The key reason? The twin deficits.

Cyclical headwinds are holding domestic demand down: Cyclical headwinds have been building. Elevated inflation eroded consumers' purchasing power, and the combination of monetary tightening with trailing weak external demand has weighed on investment. In 1Q24, even though GDP growth looks like it had held up well at 5.7%Y, the reality is that final domestic demand (consumption and investment) decelerated to 3.8%Y in 1Q24 in a broad-based manner.

Twin deficits keep the funding risks challenge alive: For some time now, the economy has had to navigate through the twin deficit challenge. To be sure, we are in no way suggesting that these deficits have only emerged recently, but it is more about the scale which has emerged post-Covid. For instance, over 2022-23, the current account deficit averaged 3.7% of GDP versus a deficit of 0.4% over 2015-19. In a similar vein, the fiscal deficit has widened to an average of 6.8% of GDP in 2022-23 versus 2.4% in 2015-19. Meanwhile, public debt/GDP shot up from 39.6% of GDP in 4Q19 to 60.2% in 1Q24.

Rates outlook – no cuts until 2025: Moreover, the Philippines has had perhaps the strongest inflation challenge in Asia. Our forecasts are that headline inflation will once again rise above the target range from 2Q24 onwards – something that the central bank has also acknowledged. Indeed, we expect headline inflation to remain above target for the third year running in 2024, which would constrain the central bank's ability to cut interest rates this year. For 2025, we do see the scope for 100bp of rate cuts as we expect that the weaker domestic demand momentum will lend some downward pressures to inflation as we enter into 2025.

Risks to growth are to the downside, risks to inflation are to the upside: We see growth risks skewed to the downside. Policymakers have recently revised their fiscal deficit forecasts wider for both the near and medium term. Efforts to consolidate fiscal deficits are likely to come naturally from cutting expenditures, which present downside risks to growth. As for inflation, we see the risks skewed toward the upside, with the trajectory of global commodity prices, especially food, and a stronger pass-through given that inflation expectations may be unanchored given the prolonged period that inflation has spent away from target. Finally, it does mean that we see the risks as being skewed toward rate hikes.

# Korea: Tailwinds from Overseas, Headwinds at Home

Kathleen Oh

(852) 2848 5200

Source: BoK, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Exhibit 114:Key forecast summary
<table><tr><td>Korea Forecast Summary</td><td>2022</td><td>2023</td><td>2024E 2025E</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Real GDP (%Y)</td><td>2.6</td><td>1.3</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.4</td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption Gross fixed investment</td><td>4.1 -0.5</td><td>1.8 1.2</td><td>0.4 0.5</td><td>1.2 2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption</td><td>4.0</td><td>1.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Contribution to GDP (pp) Final domestic demand</td><td></td><td>1.3</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Net exports</td><td>2.5 0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.2 2.5</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>1.7 -0.2</td></tr><tr><td>CPI (%Y)</td><td>5.1</td><td>3.6</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.1</td></tr><tr><td>Core CPI (%Y) Policy rate (EOP, %)</td><td>3.6 3.25</td><td>3.4 3.50</td><td>2.2</td><td>1.8</td></tr><tr><td>General govt. balance (% GDP)</td><td>-5.1</td><td>-2.6</td><td>3.25 -3.9</td><td>2.75 -2.9</td></tr><tr><td>Gross govt. debt (% GDP)</td><td>49.4</td><td>50.4</td><td>51.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>51.9</td></tr><tr><td>Current account balance (% GDP)</td><td>13.4</td><td>13.9</td><td>15.9</td><td>14.5</td></tr></table>

Source: BoK, MOEF, Korea Statistics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

An exports recovery brings a strong growth rebound well above potential this year, yet the prolonged elevated interest rate environment is set to squeeze demand domestically. Despite our upward growth revision, we see the BoK starting to remove its tightening bias to buttress weakening consumption.

Thriving exports reinforce our view of an export-led recovery story: We are revising our growth forecast upward for Korea on the back of strong exports performance in 1Q24 and an improved outlook for demand from the US and China. We now see Korea growing by 2.7%Y from 2.2%Y previously, well above the potential level. Real exports jumped 7.1%Y in 1Q24, led by the global tech upcycle and IT sector demand across smartphones to PCs, which contributed to exports outperformance over the domestic accounts in 1Q24. Plus, with our upward growth revisions for the US and China, we expect exports across nontech goods to grow faster than our previous expectations.

Limited positive trickle-down effect onto domestic demand: At the same time, the domestic economy is likely to struggle from the prolonged elevated environment and slowing hiring activities. Despite a surprise rebound in consumption in 1Q24, we see a low probability of exports recovery passing through the wider economy via labor and capex channels. With the lower employment multiplier coefficient for manufacturing compared to services, what should eventually benefit consumption capacity would be the services activities rebound, which we see facing a squeeze from the tight monetary backdrop, high living necessities inflation, and mild wage pressure.

Inflation to soften to target by 1Q25: Meanwhile, the overall inflationary environment is on its way to softening toward the target. With weakening demand-pull pressure on services inflation, core inflation has been illustrating trend inflation moving downward since late 2023. As we expect fresh food prices to stabilize by the summer on supply normalization and oil prices to remain at US\$91/bbl for this year, we see limited additional upward pressure on our inflation trajectory. Bringing down both headline inflation and consumers' inflation expectations level below 2.5%Y by 3Q24 is critical to inducing the BoK to start removing its tightening bias.

BoK to start removing its tightening bias: On the back of softening inflation and a weakening domestic real economy, we expect the BoK to start cutting its policy rate from October 2024. Despite the rebound in headline growth led by exports, policymakers remain concerned about consumption weakness due to the high interest rate and high inflation environment. Although the BoK may not engage in outright easing, we see it moving to remove the tightening bias just to the neutral rate of 2.75% by mid-2025.

Risks: The balance remains toward lower growth on an earlier and/or a weaker-thanexpected global tech upcycle and higher-for-longer US rates, delaying the BoK rate cut, and a prolonged tight monetary backdrop delaying the household spending recovery.

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Fueled by global AI-driven chips demand, Taiwan's exports illustrated strong performance, making our bullish outlook on Taiwan even more upbeat. We see continued strength in tech-driven exports, investment activity, and sentiment boosting consumption. We expect benign inflation to keep the CBC on hold until 2H25.

AI-driven exports rebound larger than expected: We are revising our growth forecast upward for Taiwan on stronger-than-expected AI-driven chips demand and an improved outlook for the US and China. We now see Taiwan growing by 3.7%Y this year from our previous forecast of 3.2%Y, versus 1.3%Y in 2023. On the back of chips demand surging on AI servers and applications, concentrated demand around the machinery and electrical equipment sector benefiting from strong US manufacturing activities helped Taiwan's tech sector. With the increase in exports of emerging application goods and inbound travel services, real exports of goods and services jumped to 10.2%Y from 3.7%Y in 4Q23. The equipment investment outlook remains solid too on the back of chips production. Our better outlook for the US and China, which are Taiwan's largest two trading partners, should boost external demand further.

Consumption on a slow moderation: At the same time, private consumption is likely to moderate in a slow manner this year. Our outlook improved on the positive wealth effect from the strong stock market performance, boosting consumer sentiment and tourism activities for 1H24, but we see limited upside factors for households' income outlook from the labor and fiscal channels. With a relatively benign inflationary backdrop and less tight monetary environment, we expect spending to continue to grow at a moderate pace, but slower than last year's strength on pent-up demand for services.

Benign inflation to keep the CBC on hold: After a surprise hike to 2.0% by the CBC at its March 2024 meeting, we see no further move until later in 2025 on the back of a relatively benign and stable inflation path around the target of 2.0%Y. We forecast headline inflation to rise 2.4%Y in 2024 with core inflation at 2.1%Y, with minimal urgency to cut within this year. Our bullish outlook remains on the external sector rather than domestic activities, which should generate limited inflationary pressure. With our US team's outlook of the Fed cutting in September this year through mid-2025, we see the CBC waiting to move only when the Fed has delivered sufficient cuts. We see no rate cut by the CBC until 3Q25.

Risks: The balance of risk remains biased both to the upside and downside for Taiwan. In case of an earlier and/or weaker-than-expected global tech upcycle, the export contribution to growth may be milder than in our base case. In case of a weaker-thanexpected domestic inflation trend, earlier CBC cutting from 1H25 rather than 2H25 could help consumption to recover faster. Higher-for-longer US rates likely has a limited impact on our base case as we already see the CBC being slow to engage in its cutting cycle.

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecast

With the global growth outlook better than it was a few months ago, this translates to stronger growth for Singapore than we projected previously. A continued moderation in inflation keeps the MAS on hold through 2025.

Better than we had expected: As a small, open economy, growth prospects in Singapore are firmly anchored towards external developments. As compared to our 2024 Outlook, our global economics team is now projecting 2024 global growth to be stronger at 3.1%Y versus 2.8%Y previously. With better growth in its trade partners, we also lift Singapore's GDP growth to 2.2%Y versus 1.6%Y previously.

External demand strength and its spillovers to domestic demand: In a way, the liftoff in growth is entirely down to a better external demand outlook and how this will spill over to domestic demand. To be sure, this has not yet panned out in the 1Q24 data, as non-oil domestic exports are still contracting by 3.4%Y as compared to -1.3%Y in 4Q23. But forward indicators are looking up – as evident in the pick-up in exports growth across North Asia as well as the sustained improvement in PMIs on a year-to-date basis. As external demand improves, we expect that it will have its usual spillover effects to domestic demand via stronger capex as well as better labor market conditions which would support consumption.

Steady progress on the disinflation front: Headline CPI inflation has moderated to 2.7%Y in March 2024, while MAS core inflation is a tad higher at 3.1%Y. Monetary policy has clearly done its job as import prices have been contracting on a year-on-year basis in 12 of the past 13 months. Meanwhile, tradables inflation has moderated to levels that are consistent with pre-Covid trends. Hence, while inflation does still sit above the central bank's comfort zone, it is clear that it is the non-tradable sector which is keeping it that way. Even then, signs point toward a further moderation as labor market indicators have been a bit softer, with unemployment rates rising a tad, while wage growth is now down to just 2.6%Y in 4Q23 from 7%Y in 2Q23.

No rush to tighten, no rush to ease: With this inflation backdrop, it should be the case that the MAS will not need to tighten monetary policy. Indeed, the MAS has also been keeping the status quo on its policy parameters and continues to sound confident about the downward descent in inflation. Conversely, we also think that it is in no rush to ease policy either. Monetary easing is usually prompted by a sharp downturn in the global economy, but that is not our global economics team's base case.

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We revise up Hong Kong's 2024 GDP by 2pp to 2.5%Y on better external demand, but the path to recovery should remain bumpy, given lingering domestic challenges of higher-for-longer interest rates, fiscal consolidation, and a subdued retail market amid a widening travel services deficit.

Better external demand, domestic challenges remain: We revise up Hong Kong’s real GDP growth by 2pp to 2.5%Y in 2024, and expect growth to return to its pre-Covid trend of 2.8%Y by 2025. The key driver would be stronger external demand led by a global tech upcycle and still resilient US growth, supporting Hong Kong’s goods re-exports and machinery capex. That said, the path to recovery should remain bumpy, given lingering domestic headwinds in the near term, including:

1) Elevated interest rates for longer: Under the Linked Exchange Rate System, Hong Kong’s base rate – the interest rate of HKMA's discount window for banks – would only ease to 5% by end-2024 and 4% by end-2025 (versus a 16-year high of 5.75% now), in line with the shallower Fed rate path. This means real market interest rates should remain close to real GDP growth, constraining investment.

2) Continued fiscal consolidation: The government has cut the F2024/25 budget deficit by \~2pp to 1.5% of GDP, and will likely stay the course of modest fiscal tightening in the following years to preserve fiscal sustainability.

3) Weaker domestic retail market, given stronger outbound travel than inbound tourism, and increased online shopping for goods from mainland China with lower prices.

CPI inflation to remain healthy at 2.1-2.2%Y in 2024-25: The key driver would be stronger private housing rental demand, with an increased inflow of immigrants from the mainland via the talent attraction program and returning expats. This could lift residential rental prices, which account for 35% of the CPI basket. That said, the subdued domestic retail demand and pass-through of lower prices would bring a partial offset.

Risks to watch: In the downside scenario of a delayed Fed easing cycle until June 2025, continued elevated market rates in Hong Kong would further drag on investment activities, and lead to a negative household wealth effect for consumption by suppressing housing prices. The delayed Fed rate cuts could also mean pushback on monetary easing cycles for most EMs, leading to weaker external demand. We thus expect GDP growth to slip to 1.5%Y in 2024 and 1.2%Y in 2025. In the upside scenario of consumption-driven China growth strength, Hong Kong's goods and services trade would benefit, given that mainland China accounts for 56% of Hong Kong’s goods exports and \~80% of the city’s inbound tourists. These would lift Hong Kong's real GDP growth to 3.2%Y in 2024 and 4.0%Y in 2025.

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Cyclically, strength in domestic demand should be complemented by an expected uptick in external demand. Structurally, investment growth could accelerate given market share gains in exports and FDI.

Robust growth momentum: After growing by 3.7%Y in 2023, the Malaysian economy has continued to go from strength to strength. Real GDP growth improved to 3.9%Y in 1Q24. Much of the growth was driven by domestic demand, and this strength is attributable to a few factors. Inflation had peaked earlier in August 2022 and that too at lower peaks. Purchasing power is improving and the drag from monetary tightening is fading more and more into the rear-view mirror. We expect that domestic demand momentum will remain strong and that this will be complemented by a pick-up in external demand.

Regaining competitiveness: How the economy has performed from a cyclical perspective post-pandemic has been very encouraging. What is even more encouraging is the unfolding of renewed structural tailwinds. Indeed, we think that the economy is regaining competitiveness. Malaysia's market share in global exports had been declining from 2000 all the way through to a trough in 2016 but since then there has been a turnaround in market share. Similarly, Malaysia's market share in global FDI has risen to 0.7% over 2021- 23, almost double the share of 0.4% between 2012-16. At the margin, we continue to hear positive announcements from companies who are investing in areas like chips manufacturing, data centers, and other AI and cloud-related infrastructure – with the most recent example being Microsoft pledging to invest US\$2.2 billion in Malaysia over the next four years.

Inflation has been tracking well: Headline CPI inflation has been at or below 2%Y for the past nine months, while core inflation has been at or below 2%Y for the past 11 months. The recent trends in inflation are a reflection of the fact that much of the inflation we have seen in Malaysia so far has been largely cost push-driven. Moreover, the moderation in inflation has continued despite the fact that the ringgit has been depreciating since early 2023, suggesting that the pass-through from a weaker exchange rate to inflation has been subdued.

Rates on hold through 2024-25: The current level of real rates, which is nearer to the 10- year average, should be enough of a restraint on inflation dynamics. Given the continuation of subdued inflationary pressures, we don’t see any monetary easing over our forecast horizon. Policymakers have committed to consolidating fiscal deficits, which we expect to unfold at a gradual pace over time.

Risks: On the growth front, upside risk could come from incremental foreign direct investment. As for inflation, the combination of robust domestic demand and potential changes to fuel subsidies should be key.

Source: CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

The economy is recovering modestly, driven by improving external demand conditions. We are awaiting shifts in both monetary and fiscal policy that will bring about a broad-based recovery.

Waiting for an upturn in macro conditions: The Thai economy has been facing a challenging backdrop marked by an over-levered consumer, real rates staying above real GDP growth, constrained external demand, and delayed fiscal expenditures. Two (external demand and fiscal spending) of the four factors have already started to turn around, while the monetary easing we expect should help to broaden the recovery.

Reduced drag from external demand and fiscal spending: Looking past the month-tomonth variations, goods and services (tourism arrivals) exports have improved relative to two quarters ago. Similarly, public consumption and public construction have acted as a drag on overall economic activity in the past six months. The lack of budgetary approval, on account of a delayed formation of a government post-elections last year, meant that policymakers could not disburse the spending they had planned for the current fiscal year – which runs until September 2024. The budget has since been approved in late March. Taken together, this means that the drags from net trade and fiscal spending would reduce over the coming months.

Rate cuts to begin from October: But in our view, the key restraint on economic activity has been monetary policy. Real rates are currently above real GDP growth, suggesting that monetary policy is currently very restrictive. Headline inflation is just 0.2%Y, after having been in negative territory in the preceding six months. While the weak growth and weak inflation backdrop makes a strong fundamental case for rate cuts, the central bank appears to be hesitant. Indeed, policymakers believe that there are structural issues which are holding back growth at the moment, which are not to be addressed with rate cuts. Moreover, the low level of nominal interest rates in a relative context and a backdrop of a weak THB will likely add to policymakers' caution on cutting rates quickly. Against this backdrop, we expect that rate cuts in Thailand will have to take place only after the Fed has cut, and this means that the first rate cut will likely be in October.

Potential boost from the digital wallet scheme: Policymakers are still pushing for implementation of the digital wallet program, which will provide eligible citizens with THB 10,000 for spending within six months.

The cost of the program is estimated at THB 500 billion (\~2.5% of GDP), with policymakers indicating that the disbursement will happen from October 2024. While we do see this as helpful in boosting consumer spending, we are mindful that the rollout has been pushed back several times, which means that the delayed implementation could pose downside risks to growth. Another downside risk to growth is likely to emanate from monetary policy – where rate cuts are delayed, which weighs on the growth outlook.

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Hande Kucuk

(44 20) 7677 3270

We see a two-speed economy, with oil GDP still hit by production cuts and the non-oil economy supported by the government via diversified funding sources to continue the progress on Vision 2030 projects.

In 2024-25 we see Saudi Arabia continuing as a two-speed economy: Saudi Arabia introduced voluntary oil production cuts of 1 mb/d in July 2023, with its production down to 9 mb/d. As a result, in 2023 the Saudi economy faced a steep contraction, with oil GDP down 9.1%Y, pushing headline GDP growth down to -0.8%Y. The non-oil economy balanced the growth with a healthy 4.4%Y. In line with our oil strategists, we keep the assumption that voluntary cuts continue until end-2025. Although volumes are expected to remain low, we do see a moderate upside revision of the growth outlook as our inhouse forecasts see oil prices at US\$89/barrel in 2024 and US\$81.3 in 2025, which is about 9.5% and 5% higher than our previous assumption, respectively. Meanwhile, non-oil GDP should remain strong, supported by the government via diversified funding sources to continue the progress on Vision 2030 projects, even if goals have stepped back. Our headline GDP forecast improves to 1.6%Y in 2024 and 3.6%Y in 2025.

Fiscal balance to remain in deficit as government capex support continues: Overall, the budget is supported by a higher oil price outlook, but we see the government continuing to boost spending on capex. The budget balance/GDP is set to remain in deficit in 2024 and 2025. The 4Q trailing budget deficit widened by SAR 9.5 billion to SAR 90.4 billion in 1Q24 versus 4Q23, bringing the four-quarter trailing budget balance/GDP to -2.3% from - 2.0% in 2023. Total revenues growth slowed to 4.4%Y (from 12.6%Y in the previous quarter), with oil revenues up by a modest 2%Y despite extended production cuts, and non-oil revenues up by 9%Y. Expenditure growth remained strong at 7.7%Y in 1Q24, with capex growth high at 32.8%Y.

External balance – the current account surplus is set to improve: In 2023 the current account surplus shrank, mainly reflecting the 16%Y decline in oil prices and 9%Y lower exports volumes in this period. Non-oil exports also declined in 2023, by 13%Y, while merchandise imports increased by 11%Y as a reflection of strong domestic demand last year. In 1Q24, the Kingdom's daily oil exports averaged 6.3 mb/d, some 1.3%Y higher than in 4Q23. In 2024 we expect the oil price to be 7%Y higher. With an improved oil outlook, we expect the current account surplus at US\$54.4 billion (or 4.9% of GDP) in 2024 and US\$60.5 billion (5.3%) in 2025.

Following the Fed: Saudi Arabia has been largely isolated from price shocks stemming from global conditions. As SAMA follows the Fed due to the USD peg, we expect the reverse repo rate at 5.25% by end-2024, with three 25bp cuts in September, November, and December, and 4.25% by end-2025.

# Turkey: Focused on Disinflation

Hande Kucuk

(44 20) 7677 3270

Exhibit 136:Growth has remained robust in 1Q24
![](images/d744604423a3afff7d164ed20beb4436bfe0ff9947a5251c599805f904856577.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 137:We see a gradual disinflation path from 2H24

![](images/47bc5691f4d69d86202ad1b7b12624f1ddcdeba97cad5d48eb627a4fb35ec0ad.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

With elections out of the way, the government's main priority is to achieve disinflation, which implies slower growth both this year and next. Lower inflation can facilitate other policy objectives such as reserve accumulation and de-dollarization, and allow further simplification of monetary and FX policy.

Domestic demand to slow from a high base: Accommodative fiscal and incomes policies ahead of the March municipal elections have kept domestic demand strong in 1Q24, despite the tightening in monetary policy (we forecast 6.6%Y and 1.6%Q real GDP growth for 1Q24). We expect domestic demand to decelerate from 2Q24 onwards on the back of the additional monetary tightening delivered in March, and expected normalization of the fiscal policy stance, as Finance Minister Simsek stated an aim to keep the budget deficit/ GDP below 5% this year and below 3% next year. We expect a bigger contribution from net exports to growth, as imports continue to moderate in response to tightening steps, and euro area growth picks up in support of exports. As a result, we see real GDP growth decelerating to 1.6%Y in 4Q (3.3%Y for 2024 as a whole from 4.5%Y in 2023). Continuation of a tight macro policy mix to achieve disinflation implies growth slowing further to 2.9%Y in 2025.

Disinflation to start in 2H24: Inflation has remained above CBT projections as of April, with the last three months' average standing at 3.9%M (SA) for core and 3.5%M (SA) for headline. The pass-through from large hikes in publicly set wages and prices in January has played a major role in the recent rise in inflation, but we expect stronger policy coordination going forward to prioritize disinflation. This, along with our projections for real lira appreciation and slowing domestic demand, implies a more notable decline in monthly inflation rates in 2H24 toward an average of 2.5%M (SA). We expect large base effects that kick in in July and August to improve inflation expectations of households and firms, which have remained high. Hence, conditional on a tighter macro policy mix, we see headline inflation declining to 43.4%Y by end-2024 and 26%Y by end-2025. Risks around elevated inflation expectations, resilient domestic demand, and publicly set wages and prices might imply slower disinflation, and require additional tightening steps.

CBT to retain a tightening bias: We see the CBT keeping the policy rate on hold at 50% until November, with a tightening bias amid upside risks to the inflation outlook and its projections (38%Y mid-point, 42%Y upper band). Following the tightening steps in March (including a 500bp rate hike) and removal of election uncertainty, the CBT's net FX position has improved materially amid increased foreign inflows and de-dollarization, while USD/TRY has edged down. But domestic demand and inflation are yet to soften, which poses upside risks to our rates profile. In the meantime, we expect the CBT to continue its active use of macroprudential and liquidity policies to enhance the overall tightness of the monetary stance in support of disinflation. While we expect the CBT to start easing steps as and when it gets more comfortable with the decline in the monthly inflation trend, which we see happening in 4Q24, it would still keep a restrictive monetary stance to ensure sustained disinflation toward 2025 targets. We think that the CBT will initially ease liquidity and macroprudential policies before starting rate cuts, to simply its monetary policy framework and reduce the reliance on alternative instruments.

Exhibit 138:We expect the budget deficit/ GDP of around 5.4% in 2024
![](images/cfce42794fdb5b3d94128b6ff53bfe23f9c649a82f7b02012cc30420066ba8e3.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 139:Fast recovery in the CBT's net FX position since local elections

![](images/feddc6c741869336c3254c9350dd9b38decb4ff719c0ec539cefc3d9365b1c24.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research

Fiscal policy stance to normalize: While Turkey's debt/GDP remains well below most of its EM and DM peers, we see normalization of the fiscal stance as key to cool domestic demand and contain inflationary risks. Recently announced saving measures aim for a 10% cut in spending on goods and services, and a 15% cut in investment spending (excluding earthquake-related parts) compared to the 2024 budget, which we expect to save around 0.3-0.5% of GDP this year. Factoring in some additional measures on taxes, we see the budget deficit/GDP at 5.4% in 2024, declining to 3.8% in 2025.

A decline in the current account deficit bodes well for reserve accumulation: Expected moderation in domestic demand, reduced demand for gold savings on the back of higher interest rates, and continued strength in tourism revenues underlie our forecast for a further improvement in the current account. Taking on board our strategists’ forecasts for oil and natural gas prices, we now see the current account deficit declining to US\$24 billion, or 2.0% of GDP, in 2024, and to 1.8% of GDP in 2025. A tighter policy mix and the start of disinflation should support foreign inflows and reserve accumulation.

Risks: Higher US rates for longer mean that the CBT might not be able to cut rates in 4Q24 as our base case stipulates. But to the extent that Turkey sticks to a tight macro policy mix and achieves disinflation, it should still see an improvement in the balance of payments at the cost of higher ex post real rates and slower growth. On the other hand, stronger global growth driven by China would support Turkish exports, and provide a cushion for growth, but higher commodity prices would increase imported inflation and slow external rebalancing.

# Egypt: A Window of Opportunity

Hande Kucuk

(44 20) 7677 3270

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Having secured large FX inflows and unified its exchange rates, Egypt has a viable path to macro stabilization. FX flexibility and fiscal restraint are key policy pillars that underlie our base case for reserve build-up, recovery in growth, and gradual disinflation.

FX inflows and a reform program in place: Following the announcement of a large investment deal with the UAE worth US\$35 billion, Egypt has delivered the long-awaited adjustments in FX and rates, paving the way for an augmented IMF loan of US\$8 billion. Additional pledges by the EU (US\$8 billion) and the World Bank (US\$6 billion) have brought the total funding package to US\$57 billion. The funding pipeline, plus a commitment to reform as signalled by the augmented IMF program, underlie our base case. Key policy pillars include a tight monetary stance to reduce inflation, FX flexibility, fiscal consolidation, and divestment of state-owned assets.

Growth to recover on the back of easing supply constraints: Removal of import backlogs and an increase in external funding should ease supply-side constraints from 4Q FY24. We expect the unification of exchange rates to improve consumer and business confidence, and support a recovery in fixed investment and private consumption despite higher borrowing costs. On the other hand, continued geopolitical tensions and pent-up demand in imports could limit the contribution from net exports in the short term. We see growth at 3.1%Y in FY24, recovering to 4.3%Y in FY25 (previously 4.8%Y).

Inflation to assume a downward trend: We expect a more limited pass-through from the rise in the official FX rate, as we have already seen prices adjust to the sharp rise in the parallel FX rate in January and February. The stability in EGP and easing supply constraints should imply a gradual downward trend in Egypt’s inflation to 27.7%Y in December 2024. Favorable base effects imply a sharper decline in March 2025 to 15.5%Y. We see end-2025 inflation around 12%Y, above the CBE's 7-9%Y target range, with risks on both sides.

Durability of FX flexibility is key: We see the CBE holding the policy rate constant at 27.25% until it starts a rate-cutting cycle in 1Q25. Our forecasts imply an ex post real policy rate hovering around 4%, close to its 2018-21 average, in support of portfolio inflows to local currency bonds. We expect the CBE to continue to allow for more FX flexibility, but its resolve could be tested in the face of a potential adverse shock.

Risks: Higher-for-longer US rates imply fewer inflows through portfolio investment, but we still see material reserve accumulation conditional on main policy pillars remaining in place. Stronger China growth should affect Egypt’s FX revenues positively through increased trade, but higher oil prices imply a wider energy deficit and push inflation higher, increasing upside risks to the CBE’s rates path, and adding to fiscal pressures.

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Georgi Deyanov

(44 20) 7425 7006

While broadly resilient, the Israeli economy is affected by the conflict via the confidence and labor supply channels. We expect a gradual GDP recovery in 2024 supported by expansionary fiscal policy. With inflation still elevated, we see the BOI cutting rates once in autumn and continuing in 2025.

Economic growth has proven to be resilient though not immune to shocks: Since October 2023, the Israeli economy has proven to be resilient given the shift in domestic focus. Consumption, investment, exports, and imports were down sharply in 4Q23 and only government consumption growth, which grew rapidly, provided a countercyclical effort. GDP rebounded in 1Q24, and we expect the recovery to continue later in the year, with the output gap closing in 2H24. While we expect household consumption to move to positive territory, we see fixed investment contracting in 2024. On the supply side, the severe shortage of foreign workers in construction, agriculture, and hospitality could weigh on the recovery in these sectors and put upside pressure on prices. Our growth forecasts are somewhat lower than those of the BOI and a touch below consensus.

Expansionary fiscal policy ahead: We expect the fiscal stance to be expansionary. Government support to displaced people is set to cushion the blow to consumption. When combined with military and security spending it is set to widen the budget deficit to 8.5% of GDP. While revenues were down 2.2% in January-April after a sharp 13%Y drop in 4Q23, the government support to the economy continued, with nominal spending up 36% in January-April, from 26%Y in 4Q23. As a result, the budget deficit widened to 7% of GDP by April, exceeding the 6.6% 2024 target.

Inflation pressures persist: Inflation has been surprising somewhat on the upside in February-April amid recovering demand and severe supply constraints. Energy is and should remain a contributing factor to higher inflation until end-2024 and in early 2025, thanks to a rise in fuels inflation. At the same time, tradables like clothing, footwear, and equipment are deflating. The BOI's preferred measure of core inflation remained close to 2%Y and we see it staying below the 3%Y inflation range threshold this year. Overall, given FX volatility, trade disruptions in the Red Sea, and expansionary fiscal policy, we expect inflation to surprise to the upside against the BOI's forecast and consensus.

The door for rate cuts to open toward end-2024 only: The security situation called for more caution this year and the BOI put its cutting cycle on hold as geopolitical risks emerged. We expect this to remain the case going forward, with the BOI focused on containing FX and financial stability risks. In addition, the fiscal stance is set to be expansionary and contribute to CPI pressures. We see the BOI resuming cuts in late autumn, delivering one cut in 4Q24 and four in 2025, bringing the key rate down to 3.25%.

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect the Czech economy to recover gradually while inflation remains range-bound within the upper portion of the tolerance band. We see the CNB proceeding with cautious rate cuts and keeping the policy rate above the 3% neutral level.

Growth on the mend: With euro area demand remaining muted in 2024, we forecast export-led Czech GDP growth to remain subdued at 1.3%Y. Yet, with the improvement in global trade growth in 2025, we expect GDP growth to accelerate close to its long-term trend. Still, the domestic demand recovery has already begun, and consumption growth should pick up in 1H24 thanks to the improvement in real income growth. That said, the pace of consumption growth is likely to remain subdued relative to the pre-pandemic period due to fiscal consolidation and tight monetary policy. We expect the government to continue rebalancing the fiscal budget, despite the general elections being held at the end of 2025.

A volatile inflation path in 2024: We forecast inflation to remain close to the 3%Y upper bound of the CNB's tolerance band until 4Q24, when we expect a temporary overshoot. After having risen to 2.9%Y on the back of higher food prices in April, we see CPI remaining relatively stable over the summer months. Yet, from September onwards a mix of unfavorable base effects and higher commodity prices should help inflation to accelerate to around 3.5%Y. Still, we see core inflation remaining relatively more stable than headline, supported by robust wage growth and a gradual recovery in mortgage lending. As such, core inflation should remain sticky but within the upper range of the CNB's tolerance band until mid-2025, when we see it normalizing back closer to the target level.

CNB to keep easing cautiously: We now expect the CNB to slow down the pace of easing to 25bp at its June meeting. We also now expect the CNB to keep rate cuts only to core meetings, starting from November. Thus, we now project the policy rate at 4.25% by yearend versus 3.50% previously. Further ahead, we expect the CNB to proceed cautiously and to deliver three 25bp rate cuts in February, May, and August 2025 for the policy rate to reach 3.50% and to remain unchanged afterwards.

Risks: In case of a more cautious Fed and ECB easing cycle than in our base case, the CNB is likely to pause its cycle after its June meeting, with the policy rate remaining flat at 5.00% for the rest of 2024. Yet, weaker demand, coupled with lower commodity prices, would push inflation below the CNB's target in 2025. In such a scenario, we see the CNB re-engaging in an easing cycle in 2025 but cutting its policy rate to the 3% neutral level to reflect the simultaneously negative output gap and below-target inflation. To the upside, stronger external demand and higher commodity prices could push inflation higher for the end of 2024, prompting the CNB to halt its easing cycle in November for the policy rate to remain unchanged at 4.50% for the entire forecast horizon.

Poland's GDP growth should still outperform peers but lower wage pressures help to contain core inflation in 2025, allowing the NBP to start with very gradual rate cuts in 1Q25.

Robust domestic demand ahead: We fine-tune our forecast and now expect GDP growth to accelerate to 3.2%Y in 2024 and 3.8%Y in 2025 against 3.4%Y and 3.6%Y previously. We still see domestic demand as the main driver of GDP growth while external demand recovers more visibly only in 2025. We expect private consumption growth to accelerate in 2024 but we see it stabilizing in 2025 due to the lack of incremental consumer-friendly fiscal stimulus. Still, investment growth should accelerate even further in 2025 due to the positive impact of EU funds absorption first on public and subsequently on private investment. We expect robust domestic demand growth to translate into imports growth outpacing that of exports, resulting in a negative net exports contribution to GDP growth over the entire forecast horizon.

Inflation to jump in July: After accelerating to 2.4%Y in April, we expect inflation to move gradually to 3%Y in June. We project the expiration of the energy price freeze in July to lead to a 1.5pp additional jump in inflation. Subsequently, unfavorable base effects should sustain inflation above the upper bound of the central bank's target range until mid-2025. Yet, we see core inflation remaining below headline inflation but close to the 3.5%Y upper bound of the NBP’s tolerance band. In our view, the weak external price pressures and moderate consumer demand will act as a restraint to the strong cost-push factors. We expect labor market tightness to ease only marginally but wage growth to moderate in 2025 on the back of a lower minimum wage increase.

NBP in no rush to cut: Despite inflation remaining relatively more contained than in the NBP's worst-case scenario, we no longer expect the NBP to ease this year as the high headline and sticky core inflation are likely to keep the central bank on the cautious side until major central banks engage in a sustainable easing cycle. Thus, we now see the NBP starting to ease only in 1Q25, with the policy rate remaining unchanged at 5.75% at yearend versus 5.25% previously. Further ahead, moderating wage growth and easing headline inflation should allow for the NBP to deliver four 25bp rate cuts throughout 2025. As such, we expect the policy rate to reach 4.75% at the end of 2025.

Risks: In our downside scenario, tighter global monetary conditions and weaker global demand postpone the start of the NBP's easing cycle to mid-2025. Yet, weaker demand, coupled with lower commodity prices, should help inflation to fall to target in 2H25, allowing the NBP to lower its policy rate to 3.75%. In our upside scenario, stronger external demand, coupled with higher commodity prices, risk pushing headline inflation even further above the NBP’s tolerance band and entrenching above-target inflation expectations. This would prompt the NBP to maintain its policy rate throughout the entire forecast horizon, ensuring that the real policy rate remains in positive territory.

Exhibit 152:Key forecast summary

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect the economy to recover mildly in 2024 before being boosted by stronger external demand in 2025. Inflation's bumpy path in 2H24 is likely to prompt the NBH to pause, but we expect it to resume rate cuts in 1Q25.

Consumption growth to lead the way: We expect subdued external demand and public investment growth to translate into weak GDP growth at 2.3%Y in 2024. Yet, with the pick-up in external demand in 2025, we see exports growth boosting GDP growth to 3.5%Y. Private consumption has already been recovering for three quarters on a sequential basis and we project it to be the main driver of economic growth this year, but we also see it moderating in 2025 as wage growth cools off. The weak investment growth reflects the government’s fiscal consolidation efforts as it continues to struggle to unlock access to the RRF and the remainder of the frozen EU funds. As such, we expect imports demand to remains subdued and to result in a positive net exports contribution throughout the forecast horizon.

Bumpy inflation path in 2024: We expect inflation to accelerate in May and to remain close to or slightly above the upper limit of the NBH’s tolerance band until August, when we see it easing slightly, only to rise again in 4Q24. As such, we see inflation having a volatile path in 2024, ranging between 3-5%Y, but we expect it to settle to the 3%Y target in 2H25. Beneath the surface, we project core inflation to remain elevated and close to the upper limit of the NBH’s target range due to sticky services inflation, with robust wage growth continuing to create upside cost-push pressures.

A patient NBH: We still expect the NBH to take a pause after its core June meeting for the base rate to remain unchanged at 6.75% in 2H24. Yet, with the inflation outlook for 2H25 improving, we see the central bank resuming rate cuts cautiously in 1Q25, but we now see the policy rate reaching 5.25% against 4.75% previously as a result of the shallower easing cycles of major central banks.

Risks: In our downside scenario, tighter global financial conditions would lead to the NBH taking a defensive stance, fearing FX depreciation. We think that the NBH would initially rely on its liquidity absorption tools to keep domestic financial conditions in check but we also think that it would eventually hike the base rate to 8.25% in 2024. Yet, with weaker external demand and lower commodity prices in 2025, inflation would fall to the 3%Y target, allowing the NBH to lower its policy rate to 4.50% in 2025. In our upside scenario, stronger external demand would boost GDP growth already in 2H24 while higher commodity prices would move inflation further above the NBH’s tolerance band. In this case, we expect the NBH to reverse some of the easing it has delivered already, hiking its policy rate by 50bp towards the end of the year to 7.25% and keeping it unchanged at this level in 2025.

# Ukraine: Normalizing in Abnormality

Alina Slyusarchuk

(44 20) 7677 6869
