Exhibit 155:Key forecast summary
<table><tr><td>Ukraine Forecast Summary</td><td>2022</td><td>2023</td><td>2024E 2025E</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Real GDP (%Y)</td><td>-28.8</td><td>5.3</td><td>3.9</td><td>5.1</td></tr><tr><td>Private consumption Gross fixed investment</td><td>-28.7</td><td>6.3</td><td>6.5</td><td>8.5</td></tr><tr><td>Government consumption</td><td>-33.9 31.4</td><td>52.9 9.0</td><td>3.7 11.7</td><td>27.9 17.0</td></tr><tr><td>Contribution to GDP (pp)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Final domestic demand</td><td>-18.0</td><td>13.8</td><td>9.1</td><td>17.2</td></tr><tr><td>Net exports Inventories</td><td>-9.8</td><td>-5.9</td><td>-2.6</td><td>-6.5</td></tr><tr><td>CPI (%Y)</td><td>-1.1 20.0</td><td>-2.6 13.4</td><td>-2.6 3.7</td><td>-5.5 6.1</td></tr><tr><td>Core CPI (%Y)</td><td>15.9</td><td>13.3</td><td>4.9</td><td>7.7</td></tr><tr><td>Policy rate (EOP, %)</td><td>25.00</td><td>15.00</td><td>11.00</td><td>10.00</td></tr><tr><td>General govt. balance (% GDP)</td><td>-25.1</td><td>-27.0</td><td>-23.5</td><td>-18.0</td></tr><tr><td>Gross govt. debt (% GDP)</td><td>78.0</td><td>85.0</td><td>93.0</td><td>94.5</td></tr><tr><td>Current account balance (% GDP)</td><td>4.9</td><td>-5.2</td><td>-7.5</td><td>-7.7</td></tr></table>

Source: Haver Analytics, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

We expect GDP to keep recovering from a low base, though energy infrastructure damage should slow the pace. Fiscal and external imbalances continue to rely heavily on external financing, which we see as sufficient in 2024. Inflation has softened materially and we see the NBU lowering its key policy rate to 11% this year.

Economic recovery continues: Despite the abnormal conditions, we see economic recovery continuing. Following the 28.8%Y contraction in 2022, GDP was up 5.3%Y in 2023. This year we see a somewhat slower recovery at 3.9%Y. 1Q24 GDP growth of 4.5%Y was helped by an exports rebound as three seaports were functioning with little disruption. Household consumption was and should remain strong. Recently approved additional payouts to military personnel and a 13% increase in the minimum wage should support incomes. The shortage of trained labor is negative for economic potential but in the near term high competition for qualified labor in the private sector should support wages. However, the attacks on Ukraine's energy sector in March-April could weigh on industrial production and slow the recovery in 2Q-3Q versus 1Q24.

A downside CPI surprise opens the door for more decisive easing: A highly saturated agriculture and food market resulted in a sharp inflation drop this year. Inflation has been surprising on the downside since January, down to 3.2%Y by April, with the 3M SAAR rate turning negative. The sharp inflation slowdown allowed the NBU to accelerate the pace of rate cuts this year. The bank cut the key rate by 100bp to 13.50% in April, above market expectations, and signaled room for further cuts if stability continues. The ongoing NBU FX liberalization is worth mentioning. In autumn 2023 the NBU returned to a more flexible exchange rate regime, with UAH weakening by 7% since then. We see gradual UAH depreciation as appropriate, as it would facilitate external rebalancing. Despite gradual UAH depreciation, with inflation now below the 5%Y target, household behavior cautious, and FX risks manageable, we see the NBU cutting rates by 250bp by end-2024.

## International aid remains key and sufficient to cover the fiscal and external gap:

Ukraine's current account deficit widened to US\$9.2 billion or 5.2% of GDP in 2023. A more flexible exchange rate introduced in autumn last year stabilized the trade deficit. As foreign aid will be weighted to loans over grants this year (and hence part of the financial account), we see the headline current account deficit widening in 2024. On the fiscal front, we expect the overall 2024 budget deficit to exceed the government target of UAH 1.6 trillion. An increase in tax collections and a 50% windfall tax on banks provided support in 1Q24, but we see fiscal performance deteriorating in 2Q-4Q24. Importantly, on the funding front, Ukraine received over US\$9 billion of international aid in March, and in April the US passed a US\$60.7 billion aid package. Overall, according to the NBU, Ukraine expects US\$38 billion of international aid this year. We see the international aid pipeline this year as sufficient to cover external and fiscal gaps and support FX reserves.

Source: Haver Analytics; Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

2024 represents a cyclical headwind in what has been an encouraging postpandemic recovery for South Africa. Improvement in 2025 should be contingent on policy continuity, monetary easing, and a pick-up in global trade.

Weakness in domestic demand has been the primary drag on GDP growth: Year-on-year household consumption is moderating off a high base thanks to tight monetary policy, a slowdown in credit extension, and slower disposable income growth. Meanwhile, fixed capital formation also seems to have stalled and may remain subdued over the next quarter or two as election-related uncertainties play out. The good news is that the binding constraint of electricity supply is finally easing, led by a noticeable improvement in grid reliability. Admittedly, Transnet continues to weigh down what would otherwise have been a more positive growth backdrop. Current rail delivery volumes are still weak compared to 2020, but the combination of recent corporate governance reforms and proposed private sector involvement on the freight line suggests that the country is past the worst of the Transnet bottlenecks.

A key determinant of whether South Africa veers in the direction of our bull or bear case is the outcome of the 2024 national election: The election is shaping up to be the most contested and unpredictable in South Africa's democratic history: On the basis of all the various poll results in 2023/24, the notion of coalition politics appears the most likely outcome. Our working assumption, based on the polling data, is that the ANC drops below 50% of the national vote and then opts to enter into coalition-type arrangements with a collection of small parties so as to form a majority government. Slow but ongoing reforms drive potential growth modestly higher over the medium term.

The path of fiscal policy depends on the election outcome: In our base case we see the main budget deficit stabilising at around 4.5% of GDP, with a revenue/GDP profile that moves slightly higher but, more importantly, a stabilization in spending. Stripping debt costs out of the accounting actually delivers a primary surplus which aids in debt stabilization. Our deficit forecasts are wider than official estimates, but are at the more constructive end of consensus.

The direction of travel for monetary policy is tricky: Inflation pressures are moderating given a tight monetary stance, slack in the labor market, and a deflationary impulse from food prices. Both headline and core inflation should therefore moderate from current levels towards target. However, there is a discussion under way regarding a lowering of the target (from 4.5%Y to 3.0%Y), which we believe could stall the base case of 100bp of policy easing starting in September. We believe that this discussion helps to explain the glaring dislocation between our policy rate forecast and market pricing, where the latter seems to have adopted the view of a target change sooner rather than later.

Source: INDEC, BCRA,, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Activity should start recovering in 2H24, but at a weaker pace on the back of muted consumption. Net exports lift growth metrics; investment should follow. There is significant progress on disinflation, as FX and monetary policy adjust throughout the stabilization phase.

On the back of weak consumer pockets, the recovery is likely to be gradual: The recession is deepening as inflation erodes consumer pockets ( Exhibit 173 ). We expect the economy to contract in 1H24 and then begin a gradual recovery. It is a consumer-led recession, although there is a sizeable contraction in government spending due to the significant fiscal adjustment being undertaken. Investment suffers this year as public capex is cut to the minimum and as private investment deals with uncertainty over the fate of the stabilization plan. Net exports is the only contributor to growth in 2024, as a good harvest lifts exports and FX reserves, and the recession (along with restrictions) keeps imports low. We expect GDP growth to contract by 2.9%Y this year. A gradual recovery should start in 2H24, led by private investment, net exports, and gradually consumption. We see growth at 3.7%Y in 2025.

Disinflation to continue: We expect disinflation driven by weak consumption and currency appreciation ( Exhibit 174 ). We are starting to see high monthly single-digit headline prints. We see price pressures fading more meaningfully in 2H24 as hikes in regulated prices should moderate significantly going forward. We do expect a faster crawling of the official FX in 2H24, including a one-off correction that could be accompanied by a cut or even elimination of the import tax, which would help to attenuate pass-through effects. We expect annual headline to close this year at \~150%Y and then around 40%Y in 2025.

The authorities will likely keep monetary aggregates tight: A policy to keep rates on BCRA-remunerated liabilities in negative real terms will likely be kept in the near term. But at some point, as capital controls are lifted gradually, real rates will have to become positive. We expect this to happen by the close of 2024.

Risks skewed to the downside: The global downside scenario has less of an impact on such an idiosyncratic story. Higher rates in the US would be a headwind for Argentina to regain market access to FX debt, but the more consequential downside scenario for Argentina would be lapses in the macro stabilization progress. Disorderly FX pressures could lead to a re-acceleration of inflation that could deepen and prolong the recession. Conversely, in our other global scenario, stronger growth in China could boost exports and lift growth metrics at the margin. Nevertheless, the bulk of the upside scenario in Argentina is predicated on fast disinflation in the coming months, allowing for improved consumer sentiment (potentially driving a V-shaped recovery) and a quicker recovery in investment.

# Brazil: Consumers in the Driver's Seat

Thiago Machado

(55 11) 3048 6249

Exhibit 159:Private consumption to be the main driver of growth in 2024-25

![](images/ff672e022d2dba133771342687e3834fdfdd7fd8bec54af1e2a38c915dbdb76f.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 160:Higher-for-longer US rates should prevent further BCB rate cuts

![](images/9cc8a65a2756e68eafc57d73e3a6a14cb7d1e1a93435b9d3cb92f405cdf10bc7.jpg)
Source: FRB, BCB, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Fernando Sedano

(55 11) 3048 6605

We see growth at 2.3%Y in 2024 and 1.6%Y in 2025, after 2.9%Y in 2023. The difference between 2024 and 2025 comes primarily from the private consumption backdrop, which portrays a slowdown on the back of still restrictive monetary policy. We expect inflation to decelerate further, albeit at a slower pace given resilient core services and a strong labor market. We think the BCB will stop the easing cycle at 10% with two more cuts of 25bp this year and keep rates unchanged throughout 2025.

Private consumption drives growth: After muted growth in 2H23, we see a more positive outlook ahead. Our stronger growth outlook over the coming quarters hinges on a solid private consumption backdrop on the back of: 1) A more resilient labor market; 2) Rising credit originations and; 3) A real increase in minimum wages. Therefore, we are adjusting our 2024 GDP growth forecast to 2.3%Y (from 1.7%Y). For 2025, we are keeping our forecast unchanged at 1.6%Y, as we see softer consumption because of a tighter-thanpreviously expected monetary policy, a product of a shallower Fed rates path. A laxer fiscal policy should compensate for the tight-for-longer monetary policy expected for 2025. The recent reduction in the primary fiscal targets associated with the higher spending recently approved by Congress for the following years points to a stronger-thaninitially expected fiscal impulse in 2025. This would bring gross debt/GDP slightly below 80%, well above peers.

Inflation decelerates, but at a slower pace: We expect year-end headline inflation at 4.0%Y in 2024 and 3.8%Y in 2025 (from 4.2%Y and 3.5%Y in our 2024 Outlook). We see core services, a measure that is closely watched by the BCB, remaining pressured above the 3%Y target at 4.2%Y in 2024, partially explained by the tight labor market and strong real wage growth. Meanwhile, food inflation should be less pressured, as the impact from El Niño has been less intense than expected. For 2025, we see year-end inflation slowing down to just 3.8%Y (from 3.5%Y previously). This is a product of a more resilient labor market next year, which should prevent services inflation from decelerating further, and higher oil price forecasts, which pressure fuel prices.

A cautious easing cycle ahead: The shallower Fed path and weaker domestic fiscal dynamics are behind tight-for-longer rates in Brazil. We see the central bank stopping the easing cycle at 10.00% this year and keeping rates unchanged throughout 2025. Our call assumes two more cuts of 25bp at the June and July meetings. Even though policymakers mentioned that there is no mechanical relationship between US monetary policy and the determination of Brazil's domestic policy interest rate, a higher path for US rates would translate into a lower rates differential, all else equal. Therefore, we believe that the BCB will need to keep rates tighter to avoid the currency depreciating more aggressively, which would pressure inflation and de-anchor inflation expectations even further. On the fiscal side, the administration recently reduced the primary fiscal targets for the coming years,

Exhibit 161:The government revised down the primary fiscal targets for the coming years

![](images/a982b4d20feaab0ef5be73c97b5be66601fe91f23773fcae1560a34d44e40a53.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Finance, Morgan Stanley Research

evidencing a lax fiscal policy ahead, which triggers tight monetary policy to compensate for it.

Fiscal risks remain the economy's main domestic hurdle: Despite the recently announced less ambitious fiscal consolidation plans, we still see targets as difficult to achieve. The amount of revenues approved so far are not enough to reach fiscal goals and, on top of that, we do not see a willingness from legislators to increase the tax burden. Therefore, we expect a worse primary fiscal result for 2025, closing next year at -0.7% of GDP (from -0.4% previously), delaying the stabilization of the gross debt trajectory.

What are the risks? A scenario of stronger growth in China is positive for Brazil. Higher upside to commodity prices, especially soybeans, iron ore, and oil (Brazil’s main exports), will likely provide a boost to the currency, which in turn reduces inflationary pressures. This, coupled with better fiscal dynamics on the back of higher commodity prices, could help the central bank to cut more than otherwise. Conversely, a potential delay to the Fed easing cycle could trigger the BCB to stop the ongoing easing cycle earlier to avoid the currency depreciating, which would pressure inflation and de-anchor inflation expectations even further. In this case, rates would need to be higher for longer to compensate for inflationary pressures triggered by the stronger dollar.

# Mexico: Decelerating Over Time

Fernando Sedano

(55 11) 3048 6605

Exhibit 163:The unemployment rate is at a record-low

![](images/d424817eb44fe8e5feab7b482d1b99d49263d97dad9d5b360573542e64cfff2f.jpg)
Source: INEGI, Banxico, Morgan Stanley Latam Economics \*NAIRU estimated by Banxico
Exhibit 164:Core services inflation remains sticky

![](images/39aa57d7888e556ef7e1069406495f268902113a3d71f813b91e76c30afe56a8.jpg)
Source: INEGI, Morgan Stanley Research

Teo Lotito

(55 11) 3048 6637

We see Mexico's GDP growth decelerating gradually over the coming quarters. A boost to fiscal spending in 1H24 and solid US growth help to drive activity for now. Yet, a weaker fiscal impulse and policy uncertainty will likely weigh on the consumption and investment outlook. Disinflation proceeds, but at a slower pace. Banxico eases policy cautiously, yet rates remain restrictive over the forecast horizon. Our main concern is fiscal dynamics; the needed consolidation seems unlikely. Risks are tilted to the downside, hinging on US policies and growth dynamics.

A gradual deceleration ahead, yet consumers and nearshoring keep GDP growth at decent levels: We expect activity to benefit from a sizeable fiscal expansion in 1H24 involving a surge in public capex and transfers to households as we head into elections. Labor market dynamics remain favorable for now and remittances continue to underpin consumption ( Exhibit 163 ). And while we look for a deceleration in government spending in 2H24, the deceleration in overall growth is likely to be limited by the healthy economic situation in the US, nearshoring-related investment, and resilient labor markets. We expect growth at 1.7%Y in 2024; we are adjusting it downwards mostly due to a lower statistical carry from 2023. In 2025, we see GDP growth at 1.6%Y as some fiscal consolidation weakens consumption and, partially, investment. The fiscal consolidation promised by the authorities next year seems unlikely. Indeed, we think that the next administration will have to use one of the escape clauses in the fiscal framework given how difficult it will be to cut programmable spending by almost 3pp of GDP next year.

Slower disinflation ahead: Solid growth results in persistence in core services metrics in the near term ( Exhibit 164 ). Sizeable transfers to households and resilient labor markets will likely keep services inflation elevated, even if we expect pressures to fade marginally in 2H24. Those pressures on services items are likely to be partially offset by continued disinflation in goods. Admittedly, a weaker and more volatile MXN and the recent rally in commodities are risks to the inflation outlook. But for now, we expect those to mostly impact non-core inflation, where the pass-through is faster. Further moves could lead to second-round effects on core. All in all, we expect CPI to reach an average of 4.4%Y this year and 3.8%Y in 2025. Pressures next year fade as growth weakens at the margin.

Monetary policy remains contractionary on the back of US-driven constraints: We expect the authorities to resume rate cuts at the June 27 meeting once the local presidential elections are out of the way ( Exhibit 165 ). Yet, Banxico is likely to remain cautious given some persistence in core services and tight-for-longer US monetary policy. The US election could lead to bouts of currency volatility in Mexico, so Banxico will have to be careful in narrowing the rate differential (versus the Fed) too much to keep MXN as stable as possible. Risks of some pause along the way are not trivial. We expect Banxico to cut some 125bp in this year (versus the current 75bp in the US). We think that the current relative stance allows for that kind of narrowing in the rates differential. Next year, Banxico will likely match Fed policy in 1H but then continue with further cuts throughout 2H, yet reaching a terminal rate that remains in contractionary territory at 7.75%, keeping the rates differential above 400bp.

Exhibit 165:We see a gradual but steady easing of monetary policy
![](images/06db84e526fe17cf5c0045211369d177c0ab8b5ac78260dfa624ae516960bd55.jpg)
Source: Banxico, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Risks are tilted to the downside: In the downside scenario, growth fades significantly on the back of a weaker US economy and tight Fed policy. Mexican exports suffer first, but investment and consumption also weaken. We expect a combination of even higher rates from Banxico, cooler growth, and lower inflation. Rates have to stay higher to keep rate differentials (versus the US) at levels that keep MXN relatively stable. We see belowpotential growth this year and next in this scenario, with lower inflation than in the baseline (especially next year). Domestically, downside is associated with deteriorating fiscal dynamics amid controversial reforms that have no funding. In the upside scenario, Mexico does not really benefit from improved China growth. Yet, better growth in China is likely associated with improved global growth, especially if US growth dynamics remain firm. So, we see growth marginally above our baseline scenario. CPI does not really break the 4%Y floor and remains high, particularly headline on the back of pressures from commodities. Banxico keeps rates even higher in this scenario, with the terminal rate at 8.75%. Locally, there are no controversial reforms on the agenda, consolidating fiscal accounts is prioritized, and energy policy fosters investment that allows nearshoring to continue.

We expect the recovery to continue on the back of improving financial conditions and labor market dynamics. Further disinflation in core inflation should encourage more BCCh easing, though a shallower Fed path should limit the amount.

The year started on a positive note: We expect better growth dynamics with a recovery in investment and consumption on the back of continued central bank easing and improving labor markets. External demand should also be a tailwind due to higher copper prices supported by China’s supply-side policies. All in all, we expect activity to expand at 2.5%Y during 2024. Favorable external conditions coupled with improving local financial conditions should provide support through 2025, with the economy expanding at 2.8%Y.

Inflation convergence is gradual: We expect disinflation in core metrics to continue over the next couple of months ( Exhibit 170 ). Goods disinflation is driven by excess capacity and low FX pass-through and should fall below target. Core services inflation convergence is expected to trend down after April when most CPI-linked contracts have been adjusted to a lower inflation regime. We expect inflation to be close to 3.5%Y during 2024 and converge to 3.1%Y during 2025.

A more cautious BCCh on inflation surprises and peso depreciation: We think that high uncertainty on the transitory nature of inflation and FX pass-through were the main drivers behind a cautious stance adopted by the BCCh. This is unlikely to change on the back of higher-for-longer US rates as expected by our US colleagues ( Exhibit 171 ). Consequently, we expect the benchmark rate at 4.75% by year-end and a terminal rate of 4.50% to be reached in 1H25.

In both bull and bear scenarios, the BCCh remains constrained by the Fed: The BCCh is well advanced in its easing cycle with rates as low as the Fed’s. Consequently, we believe that even in a more benign scenario of higher growth in China, the BCCh would still have to remain tighter for longer as this would also mean higher inflation in the US as well as higher oil prices. During 2025, we believe that easing would be resumed together with the Fed. It's a similar story for the case of higher-for-longer US rates, where the BCCh keeps real policy rates high to avoid further currency depreciation. As a result, the economy and inflation slow further and the BCCh resumes easing in 2025 along with the Fed.

Source: DANE, BanRep, Morgan Stanley Research forecasts

Fernando Sedano

(55 11) 3048 6605

The outlook improves with disinflation and monetary policy easing. However, growth is not encouraging, with relatively weak investment. The administration continues pushing controversial reforms that impact fiscal dynamics negatively; policy uncertainty remains a drag.

A marginally better growth outlook, yet still not encouraging: We see Colombia’s economy entering a recovery phase on the back of falling policy rates, a resilient global growth backdrop, and higher oil prices ( Exhibit 173 ). And while we do expect some progress on the activity front, in our view it is likely to be modest. We expect investment to be muted in the near term, despite the expected impulse from a relatively looser monetary policy. Sentiment remains weak on the back of policy uncertainty. We also expect somewhat sluggish private consumption amid a lack of strength in labor markets. However, exports should benefit from increased oil prices. We project GDP growth at 1.5%Y in 2024, rising to 2.8%Y in 2025.

Inflationary pressures likely continuing to wane: We see Colombia maintaining its disinflation momentum in the near term, with relatively muted domestic demand and the carry from the currency helping to reduce price pressures. Inflation metrics may also benefit from the weaker seasonality in 2Q, after the heavy indexation phase earlier in the year. The end of El Niño might also contribute to disinflation. Now, currency volatility is the main risk to the outlook. Altogether, we expect average CPI at 6.6%Y in 2024 and 4.0%Y in 2025. Convergence is expected in late 2025 ( Exhibit 174 ).

More cuts to come: With still-high spot rates, falling inflation, and the economy on a moderate growth pace, we believe that policymakers will keep easing policy. We expect BanRep to adopt a cautious approach amid unusually high uncertainty in core rates. We see it continuing to deliver 50bp cuts into year-end. Beyond that, we expect a fine-tuning with 25bp cuts until 3Q25. We look for a 7.50% terminal rate, which implies a reasonable rates differential with the US (\~400bp by end-2025).

On risks (more to the downside): On the upside scenario, the outlook improves on a stronger China, resulting in increased exports and investment. Congress acting as a backstop to controversial reforms could reduce policy uncertainty, while higher commodity prices provide partial relief to fiscals. On the downside, there is a combination of higher US rates, lower US growth, and further domestic policy unorthodoxy. This sets the stage for rates to be higher than in our baseline scenario. The weaker external impulse as well as the contractionary monetary policy hurt growth, which stays well below potential for longer. The fiscal trajectory is worse than in the baseline.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (o industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
